Presentations for semigroups of full-domain partitions
Luka Carroll, James East, Matthias Fresacher

Abstract
The full-domain partition monoid 𝒫 n fd \mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}} has been discovered independently in two recent studies on connections between diagram monoids and category theory. It is a right restriction Ehresmann monoid, and contains both the full transformation monoid and the join semilattice of equivalence relations. In this paper we give presentations (by generators and relations) for 𝒫 n fd \mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}} , its singular ideal, and its planar submonoid. The latter is not an Ehresmann submonoid, but it is a so-called grrac monoid in the terminology of Branco, Gomes and Gould. In particular, its structure is determined in part by a right regular band in one-one correspondence with planar equivalences. Keywords : Partition monoids, Ehresmann monoids, restriction monoids, presentations. MSC: 20M20, 20M05, 20M10.



1
Introduction

Diagram algebras are defined in terms of set partitions that come with a natural graphical representation and multiplication. Originally arising in representation theory and physics, they have become important tools in many parts of mathematics and science, including topology, invariant theory, combinatorics, logic, category theory, computer science, statistical mechanics and biology; see for example the recent surveys
[
40
,
31
]
. Diagram algebras are all constructed as twisted semigroup algebras of associated diagram monoids, such as partition, (partial) Brauer, Temperley–Lieb and Motzkin monoids
[
39
,
28
,
9
,
41
,
48
,
6
,
49
]
. Some key applications of this fact include proofs of cellularity
[
49
]
and presentations by generators and relations
[
13
,
16
,
17
,
18
]
. But diagram algebras have also ‘given back’ to semigroup theory; for some recent articles covering topics such as (pseudo)varieties, identities, Krohn–Rhodes complexity, congruence lattices, endomorphism monoids and combinatorial invariant theory; see for example
[
22
,
20
,
21
,
37
,
32
,
42
,
33
,
24
,
7
,
29
,
30
,
11
,
3
,
4
,
2
,
1
,
11
]
.

The current article concerns the
full-domain partition monoids

\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
. These were recently discovered independently in studies of Ehresmann structures on partition monoids
[
21
]
, and categorical completions of transformation monoids
[
47
]
. Both papers
[
21
,
47
]
identified natural
right restriction
structures
[
25
]
on
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
, arising from suitable domain/range maps. As noted in
[
21
]
,
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
can be thought of as a categorical dual to the partial transformation monoid
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}
. The latter is a
left
restriction monoid that extends the full transformation monoid
\mathcal{T}_{n}
by the meet semilattice of subsets of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
, whereas the right restriction monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
extends
\mathcal{T}_{n}
by the join semilattice of equivalence relations.

The monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
turns out to have a very interesting structure. Certain aspects were investigated in
[
21
]
, including its regularity and ideal structure, a characterisation of Green’s divisibility relations, links to Ehresmann categories (in the sense of Lawson
[
35
]
), and applications to representation theory. Its free idempotent-generated semigroup, and an associated topological/combinatorial complex, is studied in
[
19
]
.

In this paper we continue the study of
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
, moving in the direction of presentations by generators and relations. Presentations are key tools in many studies of (diagram) monoids and algebras, as they allow for a concise combinatorial summary of the structures, and also allow for the construction of morphisms/representations and actions from minimal data. En route to obtaining a presentation for
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
itself, we first obtain one for the singular ideal
{\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})=\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{n}}
. Here
\mathcal{S}_{n}
is the symmetric group, which is the group of units of
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
. We also give a presentation for the submonoid
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
consisting of all
planar
full-domain partitions, i.e. those that can be drawn with no edge crossings. The planar submonoid has a more complicated structure. It is not closed under the Ehresmann operations of
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
, and is not a right restriction monoid, but it still has a natural structure as a unary semigroup, namely a
grrac monoid
in the sense of
[
8
]
; rather than a semilattice, the range of the unary operation is a right regular band.

The article is organised as follows. After giving some necessary preliminaries in Section
2
, we state our main results in Section
3
. These are Theorems
3.16
,
3.32
and
3.46
, which respectively give presentations for
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})
,
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
and
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
. These theorems are proved using techniques developed in
[
10
]
for working with semigroup products arising from so-called
action pairs
, which are reviewed in Section
4
. Next, in Section
5
we cover the required definitions and results from general Ehresmann theory
[
35
]
, and the Ehresmann structure on the partition monoid
[
21
]
, ultimately identifying natural action pairs
(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{T}_{n})
and
(\mathcal{E}_{n},\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n}))
in
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
and
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})
, where
\mathcal{E}_{n}
is the join semilattice of equivalence relations. These are then used to prove Theorems
3.16
and
3.32
in Sections
7
and
8
, respectively. Finally, Section
9
gives the proof of Theorem
3.46
. This is far more intricate than the proofs of the other two theorems. It still involves an action pair,
(\mathcal{D}_{n},\mathcal{O}_{n})
, where here
\mathcal{O}_{n}
is the monoid of
order-preserving
transformations, and
\mathcal{D}_{n}
is an apparently new right regular band in one-one correspondence with the planar equivalences. A key step is to obtain a presentation for
\mathcal{D}_{n}
itself, which we do in Theorem
9.11
. We conclude with Proposition
9.45
, which establishes the above-mentioned grrac structure of
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
.

2
Preliminaries

In this section we give the preliminary definitions on semigroups (Section
2.1
) and partition monoids (Section
2.2
) that will enable us to state our main results in Section
3
. For more background, see for example
[
26
,
12
,
22
]
.

2.1
Semigroups and presentations

A
semigroup
is a set with an associative binary operation, usually denoted by juxtaposition. A
monoid
is a semigroup with a (two-sided) identity element. For a semigroup
S
, we denote by
S^{1}
the
monoid completion
of
S
; if
S
is a monoid then
S^{1}=S
, or otherwise
S^{1}=S\cup\{1\}
, where
1
is a symbol not belonging to
S
, acting as an adjoined identity.

An
idempotent
of a semigroup is an element
a
satisfying
a^{2}=a
. A
band
is a semigroup consisting entirely of idempotents. A
semilattice
is a commutative band. Any semilattice is partially ordered by
a\leq b\ \Leftrightarrow\ a=ab(=ba)
; the product of any two elements is their greatest lower bound (meet). We will also encounter
right regular bands
, which are defined by the identities
x^{2}=x
and
xyx=yx
. Clearly semilattices are right regular bands.

Green’s pre-orders
on a semigroup
S
are defined by

x\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}}y\ \Leftrightarrow\ x\in S^{1}y,\hskip 20.00003ptx\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}}y\ \Leftrightarrow\ x\in yS^{1}\and x\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{J}}}y\ \Leftrightarrow\ x\in S^{1}yS^{1}.

These induce
Green’s equivalences

{\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}}={\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}}}\cap{\geq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}}},\hskip 20.00003pt{\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}}={\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}}}\cap{\geq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}}}\and{\mathrel{\mathscr{J}}}={\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{J}}}}\cap{\geq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{J}}}}.

An equivalence relation
\sigma
on a semigroup
S
is a
left congruence
if it is
left-compatible
, in the sense that
(a,b)\in\sigma\ \Rightarrow\ (sa,sb)\in\sigma
for all
a,b,s\in S
. For example,
\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}
is a left congruence. For a set of pairs
\Omega\subseteq S\times S
, we write
\Omega^{\sharp}_{L}
for the left congruence on
S
generated by
\Omega
, i.e. the intersection of all left congruences containing
\Omega
. In the case that
\Omega
contains a single pair
(a,b)
, we write
(a,b)^{\sharp}_{L}=\Omega^{\sharp}_{L}
, and call such a left congruence
principal
.

Right congruences
are defined dually.

A
(two-sided) congruence
is a left- and right-compatible equivalence
\sigma
. The
quotient semigroup

S/\sigma
consists of all
\sigma
-classes, under the induced operation. This is well-defined because of compatibility, which is easily seen to be equivalent to having
(a,b),(s,t)\in\sigma\ \Rightarrow\ (as,bt)\in\sigma
for all
a,b,s,t\in S
.
For
\Omega\subseteq S\times S
, we write
\Omega^{\sharp}
for the (two-sided) congruence generated by
\Omega
.

The
kernel
of a semigroup morphism
\phi:S\to T
is the congruence
{\ker(\phi)=\{(a,b):a\phi=b\phi\}}
on
S
. The Fundamental Homomorphism Theorem for semigroups states that
S/\ker(\phi)
is isomorphic to
\operatorname{im}(\phi)
, the image of
\phi
.

For a set
X
, the
free semigroup

X^{+}
consists of all words over
X
, under the operation of concatenation. For
R\subseteq X^{+}\times X^{+}
, we say a semigroup
S
has
semigroup presentation

{\operatorname{\mathsf{Sgp}}\langle X:R\rangle}
if
S\cong X^{+}/R^{\sharp}
. This is equivalent to there existing a surmorphism (surjective morphism)
{\phi:X^{+}\to S}
with kernel
R^{\sharp}
, in which case we say
S
has presentation
\operatorname{\mathsf{Sgp}}\langle X:R\rangle

via
\phi
. Elements of
X
and
R
are typically called
generators
and
relations
, and a relation
(u,v)
is often displayed as an equality:
u=v
. A
normal form function
associated to a surmorphism
{\phi:X^{+}\to S}
is a function
N:S\to X^{+}
such that
N(s)\phi=s
for all
s\in S
.

The
free monoid

X^{*}
is defined to be
X^{+}\cup\{\iota\}
, where
\iota
denotes the
empty word
, the identity of
X^{*}
. We can also speak of monoid presentations
\operatorname{\mathsf{Mon}}\langle X:R\rangle
, with the appropriate adaptations.

Certain well known families of semigroups will play an important role in the paper. For an integer
n\geq 0
we write
{\bf n}=\{1,\ldots,n\}
, interpreting this to be empty of
n=0
. The
full transformation monoid

\mathcal{T}_{n}
is the set of all mappings
{\bf n}\to{\bf n}
, under composition. The group of units of
\mathcal{T}_{n}
is the
symmetric group

\mathcal{S}_{n}
, which consists of all permutations of
{\bf n}
. A transformation
f\in\mathcal{T}_{n}
is
order-preserving
if
x\leq y\ \Rightarrow\ xf\leq yf
for all
x,y\in{\bf n}
. The set
\mathcal{O}_{n}
of all order-preserving transformations is a submonoid of
\mathcal{T}_{n}
.

We will also encounter the join semilattice
\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
of all equivalence relations on
{\bf n}
. The
join

\varepsilon\vee\eta
of two equivalences
\varepsilon,\eta\in\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
is the least equivalence containing
\varepsilon\cup\eta
. The identity element of
\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
is the trivial relation
\Delta_{{\bf n}}=\{(x,x):x\in{\bf n}\}
, and the zero element is the universal relation
{\nabla_{{\bf n}}={\bf n}\times{\bf n}}
. For
1\leq i<j\leq n
we write
\eta_{ij}\in\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
for the equivalence whose only non-trivial class is
\{i,j\}
.
If
\sigma
is an equivalence relation on a set
X
, then for any
x\in X
we write
[x]_{\sigma}
for the
\sigma
-class of
x
.

2.2
Partition monoids

Fix an integer
n\geq 0
, and again write
{\bf n}=\{1,\ldots,n\}
. Also fix two disjoint copies
{\bf n}^{\prime}=\{1^{\prime},\ldots,n^{\prime}\}
and
{{\bf n}^{\prime\prime}=\{1^{\prime\prime},\ldots,n^{\prime\prime}\}}
of
{\bf n}
. The
partition monoid

\mathcal{P}_{n}
consists of all set partitions of
{\bf n}\cup{\bf n}^{\prime}
under a multiplication we describe shortly. First, we identify a partition
a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
with any graph on vertex set
{\bf n}\cup{\bf n}^{\prime}
whose connected components are the blocks of
a
. When depicting such graphs, we typically draw vertices
{\bf n}
and
{\bf n}^{\prime}
on an upper and lower row, in increasing order from left to right:
1<\cdots<n
and
1^{\prime}<\cdots<n^{\prime}
. See Figure
1
for a depiction of two partitions from
\mathcal{P}_{6}
, one of them being

a=\big\{\{1,4\},\{2,3,4^{\prime},5^{\prime}\},\{5,6\},\{1^{\prime},2^{\prime},6^{\prime}\},\{3^{\prime}\}\big\}.

To multiply partitions
a,b\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, we first define three new graphs:

•

a^{\downarrow}
, on vertex set
{\bf n}\cup{\bf n}^{\prime\prime}
, obtained by changing each lower vertex
x^{\prime}
of
a
to
x^{\prime\prime}
,

•

b^{\uparrow}
, on vertex set
{\bf n}^{\prime\prime}\cup{\bf n}^{\prime}
, obtained by changing each upper vertex
x
of
b
to
x^{\prime\prime}
,

•

\Pi(a,b)
, on vertex set
{\bf n}\cup{\bf n}^{\prime\prime}\cup{\bf n}^{\prime}
, whose edge set is the union of the edge sets of
a^{\downarrow}
and
b^{\uparrow}
.

We call
\Pi(a,b)
the
product graph
of
a
and
b
, and depict it with the vertices
{\bf n}^{\prime\prime}
on a middle row. The product
ab\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
is then defined to be the unique partition such that
x,y\in{\bf n}\cup{\bf n}^{\prime}
belong to the same block of
ab
if and only if they are in the same connected component of
\Pi(a,b)
. An example calculation is given in Figure
1
.

a=
b=
=ab

Figure 1:
Multiplication of partitions
a,b\in\mathcal{P}_{6}
, with the product graph
\Pi(a,b)
shown in the middle step.

The identity of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
is the partition
1=\big\{\{x,x^{\prime}\}:x\in{\bf n}\big\}={\lower 6.02773pt\hbox{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}
. A unit of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
has the form
\big\{\{x,(xf)^{\prime}\}:x\in{\bf n}\big\}
for some permutation
f\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
. We identify this partition with
f
itself, so in this way the group of units of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
is
\mathcal{S}_{n}
.

A partition
a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
is
planar
if it can be represented by a graph (as above), with all edges inside the rectangle spanned by the vertices, and with no edge-crossings. For example,
b\in\mathcal{P}_{6}
in Figure
1
is planar, but
a\in\mathcal{P}_{6}
is not. The set

\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}=\{a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}:a\text{ is planar}\}

is a submonoid of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, called the
planar partition monoid
. For any subset
\Sigma
of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
we write

\mathscr{P}\Sigma=\Sigma\cap\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}=\{a\in\Sigma:a\text{ is planar}\}

for the set of all planar elements of
\Sigma
. When
\Sigma
is a submonoid of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, so too is
\mathscr{P}\Sigma
.

The
domain
of a partition
a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, denoted
\operatorname{dom}(a)
, is the set of all elements
x\in{\bf n}
for which the block of
a
containing
x
contains at least one element of
{\bf n}^{\prime}
. For example, we have
\operatorname{dom}(a)=\{2,3\}
and
\operatorname{dom}(b)=\{3,4,5\}
for
a,b\in\mathcal{P}_{6}
in Figure
1
. The set

\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}=\{a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}:\operatorname{dom}(a)={\bf n}\}

forms a submonoid of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, called the
full-domain partition monoid
, with group of units
\mathcal{S}_{n}
.

3
Statement of the main results

We can now state the main results of the paper, which are presentations for:

•

the full-domain partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
(Theorem
3.32
),

•

the singular ideal
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})=\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{n}
(Theorem
3.16
), and

•

the planar submonoid
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}=\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}\cap\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
(Theorem
3.46
).

Note that the only unit of
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
is the identity element
1
, so the singular ideal is simply
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})=\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}\setminus\{1\}
, and there is no need to state a presentation for this. To avoid trvialities, we assume henceforth that
n\geq 2
.

Define an alphabet

X=\{e_{ij}=e_{ji}:1\leq i<j\leq n\}\cup\{t_{ij},t_{ji}:1\leq i<j\leq n\},

(3.1)

and a morphism
\phi:X^{+}\to\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}):x\mapsto\overline{x}
, where

\overline{e}_{ij}={\lower 6.02773pt\hbox{
\hbox to95.93pt{\vbox to30.24pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-3.50148pt\lower-1.70715pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}}&\text{if $j<i$.}\end{cases}

(3.2)

Note that each
\overline{x}
(
x\in X
) is an idempotent. Also let
R
be the following set of relations over
X
:

\displaystyle t_{ij}^{2}=t_{ij}

\displaystyle=t_{ji}t_{ij},

(3.3)

\displaystyle t_{ij}t_{kl}

\displaystyle=t_{kl}t_{ij},

(3.4)

\displaystyle t_{ik}t_{jk}

\displaystyle=t_{ik},

(3.5)

\displaystyle t_{ij}t_{ik}=t_{ik}t_{ij}

\displaystyle=t_{jk}t_{ij},

(3.6)

\displaystyle t_{ki}t_{ij}t_{jk}

\displaystyle=t_{ik}t_{kj}t_{ji}t_{ik},

(3.7)

\displaystyle t_{ki}t_{ij}t_{jk}t_{kl}

\displaystyle=t_{ik}t_{kl}t_{li}t_{ij}t_{jl},

(3.8)

\displaystyle e_{ij}^{2}

\displaystyle=e_{ij},

(3.9)

\displaystyle e_{ij}e_{kl}

\displaystyle=e_{kl}e_{ij},

(3.10)

\displaystyle e_{ij}e_{jk}

\displaystyle=e_{jk}e_{ki},

(3.11)

\displaystyle e_{ij}t_{ij}

\displaystyle=t_{ij},

(3.12)

\displaystyle e_{jk}t_{ij}

\displaystyle=t_{ij}e_{ik},

(3.13)

\displaystyle e_{kl}t_{ij}

\displaystyle=t_{ij}e_{kl},

(3.14)

\displaystyle t_{ij}e_{ij}

\displaystyle=e_{ij},

(3.15)

where
i,j,k,l\in{\bf n}
are distinct in all of the above relations, except for (
3.10
) which only requires
i\neq j
and
k\neq l
. Here is our first main result:

Theorem 3.16
.

The semigroup
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})=\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{n}
has presentation
\operatorname{\mathsf{Sgp}}\langle X:R\rangle
via
\phi
. Consequently,
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})
is idempotent-generated.

Next, define an alphabet
Y=\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n-1},e,t\}
, and a morphism

\psi:Y^{*}\to\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}:y\mapsto\underline{y},

where

\underline{s}_{i}={\lower 6.02773pt\hbox{
\hbox to70.32pt{\vbox to29.75pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-3.50148pt\lower-1.70715pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.81 23.62 M 14.17 23.62 C 14.17 24.93 13.12 25.98 11.81 25.98 C 10.51 25.98 9.45 24.93 9.45 23.62 C 9.45 22.32 10.51 21.26 11.81 21.26 C 13.12 21.26 14.17 22.32 14.17 23.62 Z M 11.81 23.62}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 35.43 23.62 M 37.8 23.62 C 37.8 24.93 36.74 25.98 35.43 25.98 C 34.13 25.98 33.07 24.93 33.07 23.62 C 33.07 22.32 34.13 21.26 35.43 21.26 C 36.74 21.26 37.8 22.32 37.8 23.62 Z M 35.43 23.62}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(3.17)

(We use over- and under-line notation for the morphisms
\phi
and
\psi
in order to reduce confusion in Section
8
, where both morphisms will be considered at the same time.)
Also let
Q
be the following set of relations over
Y
, in which we write
w=s_{2}s_{3}s_{1}s_{2}
:

\displaystyle s_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=\iota,

(3.18)

\displaystyle s_{i}s_{j}

\displaystyle=s_{j}s_{i}

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581pt\text{ if }|i-j|>1,

(3.19)

\displaystyle s_{i}s_{j}s_{i}

\displaystyle=s_{j}s_{i}s_{j}

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581pt\text{ if }|i-j|=1,

(3.20)

\displaystyle t^{2}=t

\displaystyle=et=s_{1}t,

(3.21)

\displaystyle e^{2}=e=te

\displaystyle=s_{1}e=es_{1},

(3.22)

\displaystyle s_{i}t

\displaystyle=ts_{i}

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581pt\text{ if }i>2,

(3.23)

\displaystyle s_{i}e

\displaystyle=es_{i}

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581pt\text{ if }i>2,

(3.24)

\displaystyle ts_{1}s_{2}t

\displaystyle=ts_{1}s_{2}s_{1},

(3.25)

\displaystyle ts_{2}ts_{2}

\displaystyle=s_{2}ts_{2}t,

(3.26)

\displaystyle es_{2}es_{2}

\displaystyle=s_{2}es_{2}e,

(3.27)

\displaystyle ts_{2}es_{2}

\displaystyle=s_{2}es_{2}t,

(3.28)

\displaystyle twtw

\displaystyle=wtwt,

(3.29)

\displaystyle ewew

\displaystyle=wewe,

(3.30)

\displaystyle twew

\displaystyle=wewt.

(3.31)

Here is our second main result:

Theorem 3.32
.

The monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
has presentation
\operatorname{\mathsf{Mon}}\langle Y:Q\rangle
via
\psi
.

Finally, define an alphabet
Z=\{f_{i},g_{i},h_{i}:1\leq i<n\}
, and a morphism

\xi:Z^{*}\to\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}:z\mapsto\overline{z},

where

\lxSVG@closescope {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.83449pt}{21.43584pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{32.80972pt}{21.38077pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{66.08531pt}{21.97055pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}.

(3.33)

These are again all idempotents. Also let
O
be the following set of relations over
Z
:

\displaystyle x_{i}y_{i}

\displaystyle=y_{i}

for
x,y\in\{f,g,h\}
,

(3.34)

\displaystyle f_{i}f_{j}

\displaystyle=f_{j}f_{i}

if
|i-j|>1
,

(3.35)

\displaystyle g_{i}g_{j}

\displaystyle=g_{j}g_{i}

if
|i-j|>1
,

(3.36)

\displaystyle h_{i}h_{j}

\displaystyle=h_{j}h_{i},

(3.37)

\displaystyle f_{i}f_{i+1}f_{i}

\displaystyle=f_{i+1}f_{i}f_{i+1}=f_{i+1}f_{i},

(3.38)

\displaystyle g_{i}g_{i+1}g_{i}

\displaystyle=g_{i+1}g_{i}g_{i+1}=g_{i}g_{i+1},

(3.39)

\displaystyle f_{i}g_{j}

\displaystyle=g_{j}f_{i}

if
j\not\in\{i,i+1\}
,

(3.40)

\displaystyle h_{i}f_{j}

\displaystyle=f_{j}h_{i}

if
j\not\in\{i,i-1\}
,

(3.41)

\displaystyle h_{i}g_{j}

\displaystyle=g_{j}h_{i}

if
j\not\in\{i,i+1\}
,

(3.42)

\displaystyle f_{i}g_{i+1}

\displaystyle=f_{i},

(3.43)

\displaystyle g_{i+1}f_{i}

\displaystyle=g_{i+1},

(3.44)

\displaystyle h_{i}g_{i+1}

\displaystyle=h_{i+1}f_{i}.

(3.45)

Here is our third main result:

Theorem 3.46
.

The monoid
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
has presentation
\operatorname{\mathsf{Mon}}\langle Z:O\rangle
via
\xi
. Consequently,
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
is idempotent-generated.

We will prove Theorems
3.16
,
3.32
and
3.46
in Sections
7
,
8
and
9
, respectively. The proofs require some background from
[
10
]
and
[
21
]
, which we outline in Sections
4
–
6
.

Remark 3.47
.

The defining relations in the above presentations are far from being irredundant. For example, whenever a presentation contains a pair of relations of the form
uv=v
and
vu=u
(for words
u
and
v
), the relations
u^{2}=u
and
v^{2}=v
are easily seen to be consequences. Thus, all of the relations in the three presentations declaring certain generators to be idempotents could be removed.

4
Strong action pairs

The paper
[
10
]
identified a natural class of semigroup products, and provided tools for constructing presentations for these semigroups under certain conditions. The semigroups we are interested in arise in this way, so here we review the relevant results from
[
10
]
.

4.1
Definitions and basic properties

Definition 4.1

(cf.
[
10
, Definition 4.5]
)
.

A
strong (right) action pair
in a monoid
M
is a pair
(U,S)
consisting of a submonoid
U
of
M
and a subsemigroup
S
of
M
, such that

(A1)

Us\subseteq sU
for all
s\in S
,

(A2)

su=tv\ \Rightarrow\ u=v
for all
u,v\in U
and
s,t\in S
.

Note that
[
10
, Definition 4.5]
concerns the dual notion of
left
action pairs, and allows
U
to be a sub
semigroup
(rather than submonoid); the properties
(A1)
and
(A2)
need to be (slightly) modified in this more general case; the simpler version above suits our purposes here. We now gather some information from
[
10
]
.

Fix a strong action pair
(U,S)
in a monoid
M
. The set

SU=\{su:s\in S,\ u\in U\}

is a subsemigroup of
M
, and contains
S
(as
1\in U
). If
S
happens to be a submonoid, then
SU
is a submonoid of
M
, and also contains
U
. We also note that

U\cap S=\begin{cases}\{1\}&\text{if $S$ is a submonoid of $M$,}\\
\varnothing&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

This is because from
s\in U\cap S
and
s\cdot s=s^{2}\cdot 1
, it follows from
(A2)
that
s=1
.

For
u\in U
and
s\in S
, it follows from
(A1)
and
(A2)
that
us=sv
for a unique
v\in U
, which we denote by
v=u^{s}
. That is,

u\cdot s=s\cdot u^{s}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $u\in U$ and $s\in S$.}

(4.2)

This defines a right action of
S
on
U
by monoid morphisms, meaning that

u^{st}=(u^{s})^{t},\hskip 20.00003pt(uv)^{s}=u^{s}v^{s}\and 1^{s}=1\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $u,v\in U$ and $s,t\in S$.}

If
S
happens to be a submonoid of
U
, then the action is monoidal, meaning that

u^{1}=u\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $u\in U$.}

Note that the operation in the semigroup
SU
obeys the rule

su\cdot tv=st\cdot u^{t}v\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $u,v\in U$ and $s,t\in S$.}

This leads to a realisation of
SU
as a concrete homomorphic image of a semidirect product
S\ltimes U
; see
[
10
, Theorem 4.53]
.

Each element
u\in U
determines a left congruence on
S^{1}
, which we denote by

\theta_{u}=\{(s,t)\in S^{1}\times S^{1}:su=tu\}.

(4.3)

Generating sets for these left congruences will be important in what follows. The next result gives a simple criterion for a left congruence
\theta_{u}
to be principal, i.e. generated by a single pair.

Lemma 4.4
.

If
(U,S)
is a strong action pair, and if
u\in U
and
s\in S^{1}
are such that
us=s
and
su=u
, then
\theta_{u}=(1,s)^{\sharp}_{L}
.

Proof.

Write
\sigma=(1,s)^{\sharp}_{L}
. Since
1u=u=su
we have
(1,s)\in\theta_{u}
, and so
\sigma\subseteq\theta_{u}
. Conversely, suppose
(a,b)\in\theta_{u}
, so that
au=bu
. We then have
as=aus=bus=bs
, and so

a=a1\mathrel{\sigma}as=bs\mathrel{\sigma}b1=b,

showing that
\theta_{u}\subseteq\sigma
.
∎

Principal left congruences also feature in the following technical lemma, which will be useful in Section
9
. The statement refers to the join
\bigvee_{v\in W}\theta_{v}
of right congruences, which coincides with their join as equivalence relations, i.e. the least equivalence containing
\bigcup_{v\in W}\theta_{v}
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Suppose
(U,S)
is a strong action pair, and let
u\in U
. Suppose there exists an element
s\in S^{1}
, a subset
W\subseteq U
, and elements
s_{v}\in S^{1}\ (v\in W)
such that all of the following hold:

(i)

\theta_{u}=(1,s)^{\sharp}_{L}
,

(ii)

u\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}}v
(in
U
) for all
v\in W
,

(iii)

(1,s_{v})\in\theta_{v}
for all
v\in W
, and

(iv)

s\in\langle s_{v}:v\in W\rangle
.

Then
\theta_{u}=\bigvee_{v\in W}\theta_{v}
.

Proof.

Write
\sigma=\bigvee_{v\in W}\theta_{v}
. To show that
\sigma\subseteq\theta_{u}
, it is enough to show that

\theta_{v}\subseteq\theta_{u}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $v\in W$.}

To see this, fix some
v\in W
. By
(ii)
we have
u=vw
for some
w\in U
, and then

(s,t)\in\theta_{v}\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptsv=tv\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptsu=(sv)w=(tv)w=tu\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt(s,t)\in\theta_{u}.

To establish the inclusion
\theta_{u}\subseteq\sigma
, it suffices by assumption
(i)
to show that
(1,s)\in\sigma
. But note that by assumption
(iii)
and
\theta_{v}\subseteq\sigma
we have
s_{v}\in[1]_{\sigma}
for all
v\in W
. Since
[1]_{\sigma}
is a submonoid of
S^{1}
(see
[
10
, Lemma 2.1]
), it follows from assumption
(iv)
that
s\in[1]_{\sigma}
, i.e. that
(1,s)\in\sigma
.
∎

4.2
Presentation: monoid case

The main results of
[
10
, Chapter 6]
show how to construct presentations for a semigroup
SU
arising from an action pair
(U,S)
, starting with presentations for
S
and
U
. Here we just state the relevant results for
strong
action pairs, as that is sufficient for our purposes. In our applications, certain special simplifications also arise, so we build these into the stated results from
[
10
]
.

Fix a strong (right) action pair
(U,S)
in a monoid
M
(cf. Definition
4.1
), and assume here that
S
is also a submonoid of
M
(the case in which
S
is not a submonoid will be treated in Section
4.3
).
Now suppose we have presentations

\operatorname{\mathsf{Mon}}\langle X_{U}:R_{U}\rangle\and\operatorname{\mathsf{Mon}}\langle X_{S}:R_{S}\rangle

for
U
and
S
, respectively, via surmorphisms

\phi_{U}:X_{U}^{*}\to U\and\phi_{S}:X_{S}^{*}\to S.

Without loss of generality, we assume that:

•

X_{U}
and
X_{S}
are disjoint,

•

no element of
X_{U}
or
X_{S}
maps to
1
, and

•

\phi_{U}
and
\phi_{S}
are injective on
X_{U}
and
X_{S}
.

We also fix normal form functions

N_{U}:U\to X_{U}^{*}\and N_{S}:S\to X_{S}^{*},

assuming that
N_{U}(1)=\iota=N_{S}(1)
, and that
N_{U}(x\phi_{U})=x
and
N_{S}(y\phi_{S})=y
for all
x\in X_{U}
and
y\in X_{S}
.

For each
x\in X_{U}
and
y\in X_{S}
, write
x^{y}=N_{U}((x\phi_{U})^{y\phi_{S}})\in X_{U}^{*}
, and define

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle=\{(xy,y\cdot x^{y}):x\in X_{U},\ y\in X_{S}\}.

(4.6)

For each
u\in U
, fix a generating set
\Omega_{u}\subseteq S\times S
for the right congruence
\theta_{u}
on
S(=S^{1})
from (
4.3
). For any subset
V\subseteq U
, define

\displaystyle R_{2}(V)

\displaystyle=\{(N_{S}(s)N_{U}(v),N_{S}(t)N_{U}(v)):v\in V,\ (s,t)\in\Omega_{v}\}.

(4.7)

Note that when
V=X_{U}\phi_{U}
, the set
R_{2}(V)
has the simpler form

\displaystyle R_{2}(X_{U}\phi_{U})

\displaystyle=\{(N_{S}(s)x,N_{S}(t)x):x\in X_{U},\ (s,t)\in\Omega_{x\phi_{U}}\}.

(4.8)

The next result follows from (the left-right dual of) Theorem 6.5 of
[
10
]
, combined with Remark 6.6 (see in particular equations (6.8) and (6.9) in that remark).

Theorem 4.9
.

Suppose
(U,S)
is a strong action pair in a monoid
M
, with
S
a submonoid of
M
. Suppose also that one of the following two assumptions holds:

(i)

there exists a subset
V\subseteq U
such that

\theta_{u}=\bigvee_{v\in V,\atop u\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}}\hskip 1.42262ptv}\theta_{v}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $u\in U$, \hskip 20.44434ptor}

(ii)

U
is commutative, and
\theta_{uv}=\theta_{u}\vee\theta_{v}
for all
u,v\in U
. In this case, let
V=X_{U}\phi_{U}
.

Then the monoid
SU
has presentation

\operatorname{\mathsf{Mon}}\langle X_{U}\cup X_{S}:R_{U}\cup R_{S}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{2}(V)\rangle

via

\phi:(X_{U}\cup X_{S})^{*}\to SU:x\mapsto\begin{cases}x\phi_{U}&\text{if $x\in X_{U}$,}\\
x\phi_{S}&\text{if $x\in X_{S}$.}\end{cases}

Note that the set
V
in assumption
(i)
of Theorem
4.9
need not be a generating set for
U
in general. In particular, this will be the case when we apply Theorem
4.9
to the monoid
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
in Section
9
.

4.3
Presentation: semigroup case

The case in which
S
is not a submonoid of the over-monoid requires a little more care in the set-up, but with a similar end result. Because there are a few extra assumptions, it will be convenient to state these up front. Specifically, we fix an action pair
(U,S)
in a monoid
M
, and assume that:

•

S
is
not
a submonoid of
M
,

•

U\setminus\{1\}
is a subsemigroup of
U
, and
U\setminus\{1\}\subseteq SU
, and

•

U
is commutative, and
\theta_{uv}=\theta_{u}\vee\theta_{v}
for all
u,v\in U
.

We again fix presentations

\operatorname{\mathsf{Mon}}\langle X_{U}:R_{U}\rangle\and\operatorname{\mathsf{Sgp}}\langle X_{S}:R_{S}\rangle

for
U
and
S
, respectively, via surmorphisms

\phi_{U}:X_{U}^{*}\to U\and\phi_{S}:X_{S}^{+}\to S.

We again assume that
X_{U}
and
X_{S}
are disjoint, that no element of
X_{U}
maps to
1
, and that
\phi_{U}
and
\phi_{S}
are injective on
X_{U}
and
X_{S}
. We also fix normal form functions

N_{U}:U\to X_{U}^{*}\and N_{S}:S\to X_{S}^{+},

with analogous assumptions concerning
1
and generators from
X_{U}
and
X_{S}
. Even though
1
is not an element of
S
, and
\iota
is not an element of
X_{S}^{+}
, we also write
N_{S}(1)=\iota
.

We define the set of relations
R_{1}
exactly as in (
4.6
). Note that it is possible to have
x^{y}=\iota
for some
x\in X_{U}
and
y\in X_{S}
, in which case the corresponding relation from
R_{1}
then has the form
(xy,y)
.
We also define

R_{2}=R_{2}(X_{U}\phi_{U}),

as in (
4.8
). Note that some set
\Omega_{x\phi_{U}}
(for
x\in X_{U}
) might contain a pair of the form
(s,1)
or
(1,s)
, in which case the corresponding relation from
R_{2}
then has the form
(N_{S}(s)x,x)
or
(x,N_{S}(s)x)
.

Theorem 4.10
.

With the above assumptions and notation, the semigroup
SU
has presentation

\operatorname{\mathsf{Sgp}}\langle X_{U}\cup X_{S}:R_{U}\cup R_{S}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{2}\rangle

via

\phi:(X_{U}\cup X_{S})^{+}\to SU:x\mapsto\begin{cases}x\phi_{U}&\text{if $x\in X_{U}$,}\\
x\phi_{S}&\text{if $x\in X_{S}$.}\end{cases}

Proof.

This follows from (the left-right dual of) of
[
10
, Theorem 6.44(ii)]
. Note that the set
R_{3}
of relations from the stated result of
[
10
]
is empty because the action pair
(U,S)
is strong.
∎

5
Ehresmann and restriction semigroups

The full-domain partition monoids
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
arose in
[
21
]
in the context of right restriction submonoids of Ehresmann monoids. Right restriction monoids also contain natural action pairs, as discussed in
[
10
]
, and these will be very useful in our investigations. Here we gather the relevant definitions and basic properties we will need. See
[
35
,
36
]
for more details on Ehresmann monoids.

Definition 5.1
.

An
Ehresmann semigroup
is a tuple
(S,\cdot,D,R)
, where
(S,\cdot)
is a semigroup, and where
D
and
R
are unary operations on
S
satifsying the identities:

(E1)

D(a)a=a
,

(E2)

D(a)D(b)=D(b)D(a)
,

(E3)

D(ab)=D(aD(b))
,

(E4)

D(ab)=D(a)D(ab)
,

(E5)

R(D(a))=D(a)
,

aR(a)=a
,

R(a)R(b)=R(b)R(a)
,

R(ab)=R(R(a)b)
,

R(ab)=R(ab)R(b)
,

D(R(a))=R(a)
.

An
Ehresmann monoid
is an Ehresmann semigroup that happens to be a monoid. In this case it follows from
(E1)
that
D(1)=R(1)=1
.

It is well known, and easy to show, that
(E1)
–
(E5)
imply the following additional identities:

(E6)

D(D(a))=D(a)
,

(E7)

D(a)^{2}=D(a)
,

(E8)

D(a)D(b)=D(D(a)D(b))
,

R(R(a))=R(a)
,

R(a)^{2}=R(a)
,

R(a)R(b)=R(R(a)R(b))
.

In particular, the set

P=P(S)=\{D(a):a\in S\}=\{R(a):a\in S\}

is a semilattice, i.e. a semigroup of commuting idempotents. The order on
P
is given by

p\leq q\ \Leftrightarrow\ p=pq\ \Leftrightarrow\ p=qp\ \Leftrightarrow\ p=pq=qp.

We also have

P=\{p\in S:p^{2}=p=D(p)=R(p)\},

as observed in
[
46
, Proposition 1.2]
. The elements of
P
are called
projections
in the literature, hence the notation.

In what follows, we write
a=_{1}b
to indicate that elements
a
and
b
of an Ehresmann semigroup are equal as a consequence of
(E1)
, with similar meanings for
=_{2}
,
=_{3}
and so on.

Lemma 5.2
.

If
S
is an Ehresmann semigroup, and if
a,b\in S
are such that
R(a)\geq D(b)
, then
R(ab)=R(b)
.

Proof.

The assumption gives
R(a)D(b)=D(b)
, and we then calculate

R(ab)=_{3}R(R(a)b)=_{1}R(R(a)D(b)b)=R(D(b)b)=_{1}R(b).\qed

Corollary 5.3
.

If
S
is an Ehresmann semigroup, and if
a\in S
and
p\in P(S)
are such that
R(a)\geq p
, then
R(ap)=p
.

Proof.

Take
b=p
in Lemma
5.2
, noting that
D(p)=p=R(p)
.
∎

The next result identifies a number of important substructures of an Ehresmann monoid.

Proposition 5.4
.

If
M
is an Ehresmann monoid, then

(i)

T(M)=\{a\in M:R(a)=1\}
is a submonoid of
M
,

(ii)

I(M)=\{a\in M:D(a)\not=1\}
is a right ideal of
M
,

(iii)

T^{\flat}(M)=\{a\in M:D(a)\not=1=R(a)\}
is a subsemigroup of
M
.

Proof.

(i)
. Since
R(1)=1
, it suffices to show that
R(a)=R(b)=1
implies
R(ab)=1
. But here we clearly have
R(a)=1\geq D(b)
, and so it follows from Lemma
5.2
that
R(ab)=R(b)=1
.

(ii)
. Let
a,b\in M
with
D(a)\not=1
. We then have
D(ab)=_{4}D(a)D(ab)\leq D(a)<1
.

(iii)
. This follows from
(i)
and
(ii)
, as
T^{\flat}(M)=T(M)\cap I(M)
.
∎

The next lemma gives an important kind of cancellation property satisfied in Ehresmann monoids, which provides a link to the action pairs of Section
4
.

Lemma 5.5
.

If
M
is an Ehresmann monoid, and if
a,b\in T(M)
and
p,q\in P(M)
, then

ap=bq\ \Rightarrow\ p=q.

Proof.

Since
R(a)=R(b)=1\geq p,q
, Corollary
5.3
gives
R(ap)=p
and
R(bq)=q
. Since
ap=bq
implies
R(ap)=R(bq)
, the result follows.
∎

We have already mentioned that
\mathcal{P}_{n}
is an Ehresmann monoid
[
21
]
. The full-domain partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
is an Ehresmann submonoid, but also satisfies the stronger property of being a right restriction monoid.

Definition 5.6
.

An Ehresmann semigroup is
left restriction
if it additionally satisfies:

(L)

aD(b)=D(ab)a
,

or
right restriction
if it satisfies the dual axiom:

(R)

R(a)b=bR(ab)
.

It will be convenient to have an alternative characterisation of the right restriction property. The third condition in the next result again links back to action pairs.

Lemma 5.7
.

If
S
is an Ehresmann semigroup, then the following are equivalent:

(i)

S
is right restriction,

(ii)

pa=aR(pa)
for all
a\in S
and
p\in P(S)
,

(iii)

Pa\subseteq aP
for all
a\in S
.

Proof.

(i)
\ \Leftrightarrow\
(ii)
. Given
(E3)
, note that
(R)
is equivalent to
R(a)b=bR(R(a)b)
. Since
{P(S)=\{R(a):a\in S\}}
, this is equivalent to
pb=bR(pb)
, quantified over
b\in S
and
p\in P(S)
.

(ii)
\ \Leftrightarrow\
(iii)
. With the forward direction being trivial, suppose
Pa\subseteq aP
for all
a\in S
. Also let
a\in S
and
p\in P(S)
. By assumption we have
pa=aq
for some
q\in P(S)
, so it follows that

R(pa)=R(aq)=_{3}R(R(a)q)=_{8}R(a)q.

This then gives
aR(pa)=aR(a)q=_{1}aq=pa
.
∎

Corollary 5.8
.

Let
M
be a right restriction Ehresmann monoid, and let
P=P(M)
and
{T=T(M)}
. Then
(P,S)
is a strong (right) action pair in
M
, for any subsemigroup
S
of
T
, and the action of
S
on
P
from (
4.2
) is given by

p^{a}=R(pa)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $p\in P$ and $a\in S$.}

Proof.

Conditions
(A1)
and
(A2)
follow from Lemmas
5.7
and
5.5
, respectively. The formula for the action comes from the identity in Lemma
5.7
(ii)
.
∎

We will be particularly interested in the action pairs
(P,T)
and
(P,T^{\flat})
, and the corresponding products
TP
and
T^{\flat}P
.

6
Partition monoids

Now that we have reviewed the background we need on Ehresmann monoids in general, we look at the situation for the partition monoids
\mathcal{P}_{n}
. After giving some further general background on
\mathcal{P}_{n}
in Section
6.1
, we then recall in Section
6.2
the Ehresmann structure on
\mathcal{P}_{n}
discovered in
[
21
]
. In Section
6.3
we focus on the full-domain partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
and its singular ideal
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})
, identifying natural substructures that lead to action pairs and product decompositions. Finally, in Section
6.4
we prove a number of results concerning the left congruences
\theta_{u}
, which will be used to establish presentations for
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
and
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})
in Sections
7
and
8
.

6.1
Further definitions and basic properties

A block of a partition
a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
is called a
transversal
if it contains elements of both
{\bf n}
and
{\bf n}^{\prime}
, or a
non-transversal
otherwise; these can be either
upper
or
lower
, with obvious meanings. Partitions can be conveniently written in tabular notation. Specifically, we write

a=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&D_{1}&\cdots&D_{t}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)

to indicate that
a
has transversals
A_{i}\cup B_{i}^{\prime}
(for
1\leq i\leq r
), upper non-transversals
C_{i}
(for
1\leq i\leq s
), and lower non-transversals
D_{i}^{\prime}
(for
1\leq i\leq t
). It is possible for any of
r
,
s
or
t
to be
0
(but not all three, unless
n=0
). At times we will use variations of this notation. For example, if
a
has no upper non-transversals then
a=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&D_{1}&\cdots&D_{t}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
. Or if all blocks of
a
are transversals then
a=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
. Sometimes we will omit singleton blocks from this notation; so for example
b\in\mathcal{P}_{6}
from Figure
1
can be written as
b=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 3,4&5&1,2\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&5,6&2,3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
.

For a partition
a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
and an element
x\in{\bf n}\cup{\bf n}^{\prime}
, we write
[x]_{a}
for the block of
a
containing
x
. The
(co)domain
and
(co)kernel
of
a
are defined by

\displaystyle\operatorname{dom}(a)

\displaystyle=\{x\in{\bf n}:[x]_{a}\cap{\bf n}^{\prime}\not=\varnothing\},

\displaystyle\ker(a)

\displaystyle=\{(x,y)\in{\bf n}\times{\bf n}:[x]_{a}=[y]_{a}\},

\displaystyle\operatorname{codom}(a)

\displaystyle=\{x\in{\bf n}:[x^{\prime}]_{a}\cap{\bf n}\not=\varnothing\},

\displaystyle\operatorname{coker}(a)

\displaystyle=\{(x,y)\in{\bf n}\times{\bf n}:[x^{\prime}]_{a}=[y^{\prime}]_{a}\}.

The
rank
of
a
, denoted
\operatorname{rank}(a)
, is the number of transversals of
a
.
If
a=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&D_{1}&\cdots&D_{t}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
, then

\operatorname{rank}(a)=r,\hskip 20.00003pt\operatorname{dom}(a)=A_{1}\cup\cdots\cup A_{r}\and\operatorname{codom}(a)=B_{1}\cup\cdots\cup B_{r},

while

{\bf n}/\ker(a)=\{A_{1},\ldots,A_{r},C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\}\and{\bf n}/\operatorname{coker}(a)=\{B_{1},\ldots,B_{r},D_{1},\ldots,D_{t}\}.

As we noted earlier, the group of units of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
is (an isomorphic copy of) the
symmetric group

\mathcal{S}_{n}
, which can be characterised in terms of the above parameters as

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{n}

\displaystyle=\{a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}:\operatorname{rank}(a)=n\}

\displaystyle=\{a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}:\operatorname{dom}(a)=\operatorname{codom}(a)={\bf n},\ \ker(a)=\operatorname{coker}(a)=\Delta_{\bf n}\}

\displaystyle=\{a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}:\operatorname{dom}(a)={\bf n},\ \ker(a)=\Delta_{\bf n}\}.

(6.1)

The partition monoid also contains (isomorphic copies of) the
full transformation monoid

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{n}

\displaystyle=\{a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}:\operatorname{dom}(a)={\bf n},\ \operatorname{coker}(a)=\Delta_{\bf n}\},

(6.2)

and the
symmetric
and
dual symmetric inverse monoids
,

\mathcal{I}_{n}=\{a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}:\ker(a)=\operatorname{coker}(a)=\Delta_{\bf n}\}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{J}_{n}=\{a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}:\operatorname{dom}(a)=\operatorname{codom}(a)={\bf n}\}.

A partition
a
from
\mathcal{T}_{n}
is identified with the mapping
{\bf n}\to{\bf n}
that sends
x
to
y
where
y^{\prime}
is the unique element of
{\bf n}^{\prime}
belonging to the block of
a
containing
x
. A typical element of
\mathcal{T}_{n}
has the tabular form
\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil b_{1}&\cdots&b_{r}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
, where we recall the above convention about omitting singleton blocks.

Elements of
\mathcal{J}_{n}
are called
block bijections
, and are the partitions from
\mathcal{P}_{n}
whose blocks are all transversals. Such a block bijection
a\in\mathcal{J}_{n}
is
uniform
if every transversal
A\cup B^{\prime}
of
a
satisfies
|A|=|B|
. The set

\mathcal{F}_{n}=\{a\in\mathcal{J}_{n}:a\text{ is uniform}\}

(6.3)

is a submonoid of
\mathcal{J}_{n}
, and hence of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
. The monoid
\mathcal{F}_{n}
will play an important role in Section
8
.

6.2
Ehresmann structure

For an equivalence
\varepsilon\in\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
, with
{\bf n}/\varepsilon=\{A_{1},\ldots,A_{r}\}
, we define
\operatorname{id}_{\varepsilon}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
. Note that all blocks of
\operatorname{id}_{\varepsilon}
are transversals. It is easy to see that

\operatorname{id}_{\varepsilon}\operatorname{id}_{\eta}=\operatorname{id}_{\varepsilon\vee\eta}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $\varepsilon,\eta\in\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}$.}

In particular, the set

\mathcal{E}_{n}=\{\operatorname{id}_{\varepsilon}:\varepsilon\in\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}\}

is a submonoid of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, and is isomorphic to the join semilattice
(\mathfrak{Eq}_{n},\vee)
. Note that
\operatorname{id}_{\Delta_{{\bf n}}}=1
is the identity element of both
\mathcal{E}_{n}
and
\mathcal{P}_{n}
.

For
a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, we define

D(a)=\operatorname{id}_{\ker(a)}\and R(a)=\operatorname{id}_{\operatorname{coker}(a)}.

(6.4)

In tabular notation, if
a=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&D_{1}&\cdots&D_{t}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
, then

D(a)=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\and R(a)=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&D_{1}&\cdots&D_{t}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&D_{1}&\cdots&D_{t}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

(6.5)

It follows from
[
21
, Theorem 4.8]
that these operations give
\mathcal{P}_{n}
the structure of an Ehresmann monoid, i.e. that
(E1)
–
(E5)
hold. See also
[
38
]
. Note then that the set

P(\mathcal{P}_{n})=\{D(a):a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\}=\{R(a):a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\}

is precisely the semilattice
\mathcal{E}_{n}
.

6.3
The full-domain partition monoid

The Ehresmann monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}
is neither left nor right restriction when
n\geq 2
. For example, it is easy to check that
(L)
and
(R)
both fail for the partitions
a={\lower 6.02773pt\hbox{

}}
and
b={\lower 6.02773pt\hbox{

{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 23.62 23.62 L 23.62 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{}
{}
{}
{}{}{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{}{}{}{}}}}{}
{}
{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.81 23.62 C 11.81 21.01 13.93 18.9 16.54 18.9 M 16.54 18.9 L 18.9 18.9 M 18.9 18.9 C 21.51 18.9 23.62 21.01 23.62 23.62}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{}

{}
