{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{}{}{}{}}}}{}
{}
{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.81 0 C 11.81 2.61 13.93 4.72 16.54 4.72 M 16.54 4.72 L 18.9 4.72 M 18.9 4.72 C 21.51 4.72 23.62 2.61 23.62 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}
from
\mathcal{P}_{2}
.

On the other hand,
\mathcal{P}_{n}
contains a very natural right restriction Ehresmann submonoid, namely the
full-domain partition monoid:

\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}=\{a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}:\operatorname{dom}(a)={\bf n}\},

which will be the focus of our study.
(Dually, the full-codomain partition monoid is a left restriction Ehresmann monoid.) Note that
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
contains the semilattice
\mathcal{E}_{n}
and the group
\mathcal{S}_{n}
. Several structural results concerning
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
were proved in
[
21
]
, including a description of Green’s relations, and establishing its regularity and the right restriction property.

The right restriction monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
contains the natural action pairs
(P,T)
and
(P,T^{\flat})
, where

P=P(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})=\mathcal{E}_{n},\hskip 20.00003ptT=T(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})\and T^{\flat}=T^{\flat}(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}),

are the substructures from Proposition
5.4
. The next result characterises these substructures, and also identifies the resulting products
TP
and
T^{\flat}P
.

Proposition 6.6
.

We have

(i)

T(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})=\mathcal{T}_{n}
,

(ii)

I(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})=\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{n}
,

(iii)

T^{\flat}(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})=\mathcal{T}_{n}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{n}
.

Further, we have the product decompositions

\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}=\mathcal{T}_{n}\mathcal{E}_{n}\and\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})\mathcal{E}_{n}.

Proof.

(i)
. By definition, and using (
6.2
), we have

\displaystyle T(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})

\displaystyle=\{a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}:R(a)=1\}

\displaystyle=\{a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}:\operatorname{coker}(a)=\Delta_{\bf n}\}

\displaystyle=\{a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}:\operatorname{dom}(a)={\bf n},\ \operatorname{coker}(a)=\Delta_{\bf n}\}=\mathcal{T}_{n}.

(ii)
. By definition, and using (
6.1
), we have

\displaystyle I(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})

\displaystyle=\{a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}:D(a)\not=1\}=\{a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}:\operatorname{dom}(a)={\bf n},\ \ker(a)\not=\Delta_{\bf n}\}=\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{n}.

(iii)
. This follows from
(i)
and
(ii)
, since
T^{\flat}(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})=T(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})\cap I(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})
.

For the final assertion, let
a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
, and write
a=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&D_{1}&\cdots&D_{t}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
. For each
1\leq i\leq r
, fix some
b_{i}\in B_{i}
, and let
b=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil b_{1}&\cdots&b_{r}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\in\mathcal{T}_{n}
. Keeping (
6.5
) in mind, it is then easy to see that

a=bR(a)\in\mathcal{T}_{n}\mathcal{E}_{n}.

If
a
belongs to
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})
, then
\operatorname{rank}(b)=\operatorname{rank}(a)<n
, so in fact
b\in\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})
.
∎

Remark 6.7
.

The monoid
\mathcal{F}_{n}
in (
6.3
) also has a product decomposition, as
\mathcal{F}_{n}=\mathcal{E}_{n}\mathcal{S}_{n}=\mathcal{S}_{n}\mathcal{E}_{n}
.

Remark 6.8
.

Green’s relations on
\mathcal{P}_{n}
have simple descriptions in terms of co/domain, co/kernel and rank parameters; see for example
[
49
,
24
]
. In particular, for partitions
a,b\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
we have

a\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}b\hskip 20.00003pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 20.00003pt\operatorname{dom}(a)=\operatorname{dom}(b)\text{ and }\ker(a)=\ker(b).

(6.9)

It follows that the elements of
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
can be characterised as the partitions
\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}
-related to an element of
\mathcal{E}_{n}=P(\mathcal{P}_{n})
. Note, however, that for an arbitrary Ehresmann semigroup
S
, the set

\{a\in S:a\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}p\ (\exists p\in P(S))\}=\{a\in S:a\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}D(a)\}

need not be a subsemigroup of
S
in general. Indeed, small (counter)examples can be quickly found using Mace4
[
43
]
. On the other hand, the set

\{a\in S:D(a)\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}a\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}R(a)\}

is an
inverse
subsemigroup, as shown in
[
45
, Lemma 5.15]
.

6.4
The left congruences

The next three results concern the left congruences
\theta_{u}
(
u\in\mathcal{E}_{n}
) from (
4.3
) associated to the action pairs
(P,T)=(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{T}_{n})
and
(P,T^{\flat})=(\mathcal{E}_{n},\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n}))
in
M=\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
.

•

Lemma
6.10
characterises the congruences themselves.

•

Lemma
6.11
shows that the congruences have the required properties in order to apply Theorems
4.9
and
4.10
.

•

Lemma
6.14
gives a generating set for the congruences
\theta_{u}
for certain simple elements
u
.

Lemma 6.10
.

If
S=\mathcal{T}_{n}
or
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})
, and if
u=\operatorname{id}_{\varepsilon}
for some
\varepsilon\in\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
, then

\theta_{u}=\{(a,b)\in S^{1}\times S^{1}:(xa,xb)\in\varepsilon\ \text{ for all }x\in{\bf n}\}.

Proof.

Let
a,b\in S^{1}
, and write
{\bf n}/\varepsilon=\{A_{1},\ldots,A_{k}\}
. We then have

au=\{A_{i}a^{-1}\cup A_{i}^{\prime}:i\in{\bf k}\},

where as usual we write
A_{i}a^{-1}=\{x\in{\bf n}:xa\in A_{i}\}
. It follows that

(a,b)\in\theta_{u}\hskip 20.00003pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 20.00003ptau=bu\hskip 20.00003pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 20.00003ptA_{i}a^{-1}=A_{i}b^{-1}\text{ for all $i\in{\bf k}$.}

It is easy to see that this latter condition is equivalent to having
(xa,xb)\in\varepsilon
for all
x\in{\bf n}
.
∎

Lemma 6.11
.

If
S=\mathcal{T}_{n}
or
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})
, then

\theta_{uv}=\theta_{u}\vee\theta_{v}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $u,v\in\mathcal{E}_{n}$.}

Proof.

Let
u=\operatorname{id}_{\varepsilon}
and
v=\operatorname{id}_{\eta}
, where
\varepsilon,\eta\in\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
. Since
\mathcal{E}_{n}
is commutative, we have
\theta_{u}\vee\theta_{v}\subseteq\theta_{uv}
(see
[
10
, p. 52]
), so it remains to show that

\theta_{uv}\subseteq\theta_{u}\vee\theta_{v}.

(6.12)

To do so, let
(a,b)\in\theta_{uv}
. Since
uv=\operatorname{id}_{\varepsilon\vee\eta}
, it follows from Lemma
6.10
that
(xa,xb)\in\varepsilon\vee\eta
for all
x\in{\bf n}
. We therefore have sequences

\begin{matrix}1a&=&h_{1,0}&\mathrel{\varepsilon}&h_{1,1}&\mathrel{\eta}&h_{1,2}&\mathrel{\varepsilon}&\cdots&\mathrel{\eta}&h_{1,k}&=&1b,\\
2a&=&h_{2,0}&\mathrel{\varepsilon}&h_{2,1}&\mathrel{\eta}&h_{2,2}&\mathrel{\varepsilon}&\cdots&\mathrel{\eta}&h_{2,k}&=&2b,\\
\vdots&&&&&&&&&&&&\vdots\\
xa&=&h_{x,0}&\mathrel{\varepsilon}&h_{x,1}&\mathrel{\eta}&h_{x,2}&\mathrel{\varepsilon}&\cdots&\mathrel{\eta}&h_{x,k}&=&xb,\\
\vdots&&&&&&&&&&&&\vdots\\
na&=&h_{n,0}&\mathrel{\varepsilon}&h_{n,1}&\mathrel{\eta}&h_{n,2}&\mathrel{\varepsilon}&\cdots&\mathrel{\eta}&h_{n,k}&=&nb.\end{matrix}

(6.13)

Since
\varepsilon
and
\eta
are reflexive, we can indeed assume that the above sequences have the same length, and that they alternate between
\varepsilon
- and
\eta
-relationships as shown.
Moreover, if we have
xa=ya
and
xb=yb
for some
x,y\in{\bf n}
, then we assume that the sequences in rows
x
and
y
of (
6.13
) are identical.
We then use the above columns to define a sequence of transformations
c_{0},c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}\in\mathcal{T}_{n}
. Specifically, for
0\leq j\leq k
, we define

xc_{j}=h_{x,j}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for each $x\in{\bf n}$.}

If each
c_{j}
belongs to
S^{1}
, then it again follows from Lemma
6.10
that

(c_{j},c_{j+1})\in\begin{cases}\theta_{u}&\text{if $j$ is even,}\\
\theta_{v}&\text{if $j$ is odd.}\end{cases}

Thus, in this case we have

\begin{matrix}a&=&c_{0}&\mathrel{\theta_{u}}&c_{1}&\mathrel{\theta_{v}}&c_{2}&\mathrel{\theta_{u}}&\cdots&\mathrel{\theta_{v}}&c_{k}&=&b,\end{matrix}

meaning that
(a,b)\in\theta_{u}\vee\theta_{v}
, completing the proof of (
6.12
). In particular, there is nothing more to be done in the case that
S=\mathcal{T}_{n}(=S^{1})
.

Thus, for the rest of the proof we assume that
S=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})
, and deal with the case that some transformation
c_{j}
constructed above does not belong to
S^{1}=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})\cup\{1\}
. In this case we have
c_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}\setminus\{1\}
, but we note that
a,b\in S^{1}
. Now, if
a=b=1
, then clearly
(a,b)\in\theta_{u}\vee\theta_{v}
. So we assume without loss of generality that
b\not=1
. In particular,
b
is not injective, so without loss of generality we can assume that
1b=2b
.

Case 1.
If we also had
1a=2a
, then by our assumption above, rows
1
and
2
of (
6.13
) would be identical. This would imply that
1c_{j}=2c_{j}
for each
0\leq j\leq k
, i.e. that each
c_{j}\in\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})\subseteq S^{1}
, completing the proof in this case.

Case 2.
We are now left to deal with the case that
1a\not=2a
. Swapping
1
and
2
if necessary, we can assume that either
a=1
is the identity map, or else there exist distinct
y,z\in\{2,\ldots,n\}
such that
ya=za
. Since
(1a,1b)
and
(2a,2b)
are both in
\varepsilon\vee\eta
, and since
1b=2b
, it follows that
(1a,2a)\in\varepsilon\vee\eta
. We therefore have a sequence

\begin{matrix}1a&=&h_{0}&\mathrel{\varepsilon}&h_{1}&\mathrel{\eta}&h_{2}&\mathrel{\varepsilon}&\cdots&\mathrel{\eta}&h_{l}&=&2a.\end{matrix}

We use this to define another sequence of transformations
d_{0},d_{1},\ldots,d_{l}\in\mathcal{T}_{n}
. For
0\leq j\leq l
, we define

xd_{j}=\begin{cases}h_{j}&\text{if $x=1$,}\\
xa&\text{if $2\leq x\leq n$,}\end{cases}

and we note that each
d_{j}\in S^{1}=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})\cup\{1\}
. Indeed, if
a=1
, then
d_{j}
is either
1
(if
h_{j}=1
) or else singular (as then
1d_{j}=h_{j}=h_{j}d_{j}
). On the other hand, if
a\not=1
, then with
2\leq y<z\leq n
as above, we have
yd_{j}=ya=za=zd_{j}
, so that
d_{j}
is singular. We now define
a^{\prime}=d_{l}
. Another appeal to Lemma
6.10
gives

\begin{matrix}a&=&d_{0}&\mathrel{\theta_{u}}&d_{1}&\mathrel{\theta_{v}}&d_{2}&\mathrel{\theta_{u}}&\cdots&\mathrel{\theta_{v}}&d_{l}&=&a^{\prime},\end{matrix}

so that
(a,a^{\prime})\in\theta_{u}\vee\theta_{v}
. Since
(a^{\prime},a)\in\theta_{u}\vee\theta_{v}\subseteq\theta_{uv}
and
(a,b)\in\theta_{uv}
, it follows by transitivity that
(a^{\prime},b)\in\theta_{uv}
. Since
1a^{\prime}=2a^{\prime}
and
1b=2b
, we see that
a^{\prime}
and
b
satisfy the conditions of Case 1, so it follows that
(a^{\prime},b)\in\theta_{u}\vee\theta_{v}
. Combining this with
(a,a^{\prime})\in\theta_{u}\vee\theta_{v}
and transitivity, it follows that indeed
(a,b)\in\theta_{u}\vee\theta_{v}
. This completes the proof.
∎

The next result refers to the partitions
\overline{e}_{ij}
and
\overline{t}_{ij}
from (
3.2
), and the proof uses the equivalences
\eta_{ij}\in\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
introduced at the end of Section
2.1
.

Lemma 6.14
.

If
S=\mathcal{T}_{n}
or
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})
, and if
\varepsilon\in\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
, then
\theta_{\operatorname{id}_{\varepsilon}}=(1,f)^{\sharp}_{L}
for any idempotent
f\in E(\mathcal{T}_{n})
with
\ker(f)=\varepsilon
.
In particular, for any
1\leq i<j\leq n
we have
\theta_{\overline{e}_{ij}}=(1,\overline{t}_{ij})^{\sharp}_{L}
.

Proof.

The first assertion follows from Lemma
4.4
, since
f\operatorname{id}_{\varepsilon}=\operatorname{id}_{\varepsilon}
and
\operatorname{id}_{\varepsilon}f=f
.
The second follows from the first, since
\overline{e}_{ij}=\operatorname{id}_{\eta_{ij}}
and
\ker(\overline{t}_{ij})=\eta_{ij}
.
∎

7
Presentation for
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})

To prove Theorem
3.16
, we now work towards an application of Theorem
4.10
, utilising the strong action pair
(P,T^{\flat})=(\mathcal{E}_{n},\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n}))
in the right restriction monoid
M=\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
. First we note that:

•

\mathcal{E}_{n}\setminus\{1\}
is a subsemigroup of
\mathcal{E}_{n}
(as is the case for any monoid semilattice),

•

\mathcal{E}_{n}\setminus\{1\}\subseteq\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})\mathcal{E}_{n}
(cf. Proposition
6.6
),

•

\mathcal{E}_{n}
is commutative, and
\theta_{uv}=\theta_{u}\vee\theta_{v}
for all
u,v\in\mathcal{E}_{n}
(cf. Lemma
6.11
).

Thus, Theorem
4.10
does indeed apply. To use it, we need presentations
\operatorname{\mathsf{Mon}}\langle X_{\mathcal{E}_{n}}:R_{\mathcal{E}_{n}}\rangle
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{Sgp}}\langle X_{\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})}:R_{\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})}\rangle
for
\mathcal{E}_{n}
and
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})
, via appropriate surmorphisms. By
[
23
, Theorem 2]
and
[
14
, Theorem 6]
, such presentations exist with:

•

X_{\mathcal{E}_{n}}=\{e_{ij}=e_{ji}:1\leq i<j\leq n\}
, and
R_{\mathcal{E}_{n}}
the set of relations (
3.9
)–(
3.11
),

•

X_{\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})}=\{t_{ij},t_{ji}:1\leq i<j\leq n\}
, and
R_{\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})}
the set of relations (
3.3
)–(
3.8
).

Noting that
X_{\mathcal{E}_{n}}\cup X_{\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})}
is the alphabet
X
from (
3.1
), Theorem
4.10
then tells us that
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})\mathcal{E}_{n}
has presentation

\operatorname{\mathsf{Sgp}}\langle X:R_{\mathcal{E}_{n}}\cup R_{\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{2}\rangle,

(7.1)

where
R_{1}
is as in (
4.6
), and where
R_{2}=R_{2}(X_{U}\phi_{U})
is as in (
4.8
). Given Lemma
6.14
, we can take
R_{2}
to consist of relations (
3.15
). For
R_{1}
, we need words
e_{kl}^{t_{ij}}\in X_{\mathcal{E}_{n}}^{*}
that map to
\overline{e}_{kl}^{\overline{t}_{ij}}=R(\overline{e}_{kl}\overline{t}_{ij})
. One can easily check that

\overline{e}_{kl}^{\overline{t}_{ij}}=R(\overline{e}_{kl}\overline{t}_{ij})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $k=i$ and $l=j$,}\\
1&\text{if $k=j$ and $l=i$,}\\
\overline{e}_{il}&\text{if $k=j$ and $l\not=i$,}\\
\overline{e}_{ik}&\text{if $l=j$ and $k\not=i$,}\\
\overline{e}_{kl}&\text{if $\{i,j\}\cap\{k,l\}=\varnothing$,}\\
\overline{e}_{il}&\text{if $k=i$ and $l\not=j$,}\\
\overline{e}_{ki}&\text{if $l=i$ and $k\not=j$.}\end{cases}

In these seven cases, and with the obvious choice of words
e_{kl}^{t_{ij}}
(being empty or a single letter), the resulting relations
e_{kl}t_{ij}=t_{ij}e_{kl}^{t_{ij}}
from
R_{1}
respectively become

\displaystyle e_{ij}t_{ij}

\displaystyle=t_{ij}

for distinct
i,j\in{\bf n}
,

(7.2)

\displaystyle e_{ji}t_{ij}

\displaystyle=t_{ij}

for distinct
i,j\in{\bf n}
,

(7.3)

\displaystyle e_{jl}t_{ij}

\displaystyle=t_{ij}e_{il}

for distinct
i,j,l\in{\bf n}
,

(7.4)

\displaystyle e_{kj}t_{ij}

\displaystyle=t_{ij}e_{ik}

for distinct
i,j,k\in{\bf n}
,

(7.5)

\displaystyle e_{kl}t_{ij}

\displaystyle=t_{ij}e_{kl}

for distinct
i,j,k,l\in{\bf n}
,

(7.6)

\displaystyle e_{il}t_{ij}

\displaystyle=t_{ij}e_{il}

for distinct
i,j,l\in{\bf n}
,

(7.7)

\displaystyle e_{ki}t_{ij}

\displaystyle=t_{ij}e_{ki}

for distinct
i,j,k\in{\bf n}
.

(7.8)

Keeping in mind the symmetric notation
e_{ij}=e_{ji}
, we see that:

•

(
7.2
) and (
7.3
) are the same sets of relations, and are exactly (
3.12
),

•

(
7.4
) and (
7.5
) are the same sets of relations, and are exactly (
3.13
),

•

(
7.6
) is exactly (
3.14
),

•

(
7.7
) and (
7.8
) are the same sets of relations, but are not included in the set
R
of relations (
3.3
)–(
3.15
).

Thus, the presentation for
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
in (
7.1
) contains the presentation
\operatorname{\mathsf{Sgp}}\langle X:R\rangle
from Theorem
3.16
plus relations (
7.8
). It therefore remains to show that relations (
7.8
) follow from
R
. For this we see that for distinct
i,j,k\in{\bf n}
we can transform

e_{ki}t_{ij}\to_{\eqref{ET1}}e_{ki}e_{ij}t_{ij}\to_{\eqref{EE3}}e_{ij}e_{jk}t_{ij}\to_{\eqref{ET2}}e_{ij}t_{ij}e_{ik}\to_{\eqref{ET1}}t_{ij}e_{ik}=t_{ij}e_{ki}.

This completes the proof of Theorem
3.16
.

8
Presentation for
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}

There are several ways one could prove Theorem
3.32
:

•

We could apply Theorem
4.9
, utilising the strong action pair
(P,T)=(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{T}_{n})
in the right restriction monoid
M=\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}=\mathcal{T}_{n}\mathcal{E}_{n}
, again using Lemma
6.11
to verify the key assumption on joins of left congruences. This would require a presentation
\operatorname{\mathsf{Mon}}\langle X_{\mathcal{T}_{n}}:R_{\mathcal{T}_{n}}\rangle
for
\mathcal{T}_{n}
(see for example
[
34
, Theorem 9]
), and would result in a presentation for
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
over the alphabet
X_{\mathcal{E}_{n}}\cup X_{\mathcal{T}_{n}}
, which would then have to be transformed into
\operatorname{\mathsf{Mon}}\langle Y:Q\rangle
.

•

We could apply
[
15
, Theorem 7.1]
, which allows us to build a presentation for
{\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}=\mathcal{S}_{n}\cup\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})}
from presentations for
\mathcal{S}_{n}
and
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})
, using the fact that
\mathcal{S}_{n}
is a sub(semi)group of
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
, and
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})
an ideal. This would again involve a rather large generating set, and require a transformation.

•

We could use the approach of
[
17
,
16
]
, where we begin with the desired presentation
{\operatorname{\mathsf{Mon}}\langle Y:Q\rangle}
, and in some sense ‘simulate’ the presentation
\operatorname{\mathsf{Sgp}}\langle X:R\rangle
inside
\operatorname{\mathsf{Mon}}\langle Y:Q\rangle
.

We take the latter approach here, as it appears to be the most direct.

For the rest of this section, we fix
the alphabet
Y=\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n-1},e,t\}
,
the set
Q
of relations (
3.18
)–(
3.31
), and
the morphism
\psi:Y^{*}\to\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}:y\mapsto\underline{y}
, as in (
3.17
).
For
w\in Y^{*}
we write
\underline{w}=w\psi\in\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
. We also write
{\sim}=Q^{\sharp}
for the congruence on
Y^{*}
generated by the relations
Q
. When demonstrating
\sim
-equivalence of words, we use subscripts to indicate which relation we are using.

We now define three sub-alphabets of
Q
:

X_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}=\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n-1}\},\hskip 20.00003ptX_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}=X_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}\cup\{e\}\and X_{\mathcal{T}_{n}}=X_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}\cup\{t\}.

Lemma 8.1
.

(i)

For any
u,v\in X_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}^{*}
we have
\underline{u}=\underline{v}\ \Rightarrow\ u\sim v
.

(ii)

For any
u,v\in X_{\mathcal{T}_{n}}^{*}
we have
\underline{u}=\underline{v}\ \Rightarrow\ u\sim v
.

Proof.

By
[
23
, Theorem 3]
and
[
34
, Theorem 9]
, the monoids
\mathcal{F}_{n}
and
\mathcal{T}_{n}
have presentations

\operatorname{\mathsf{Mon}}\langle X_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}:R_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}\rangle\and\operatorname{\mathsf{Mon}}\langle X_{\mathcal{T}_{n}}:R_{\mathcal{T}_{n}}\rangle

via the restrictions
\psi{\upharpoonright}_{X_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}^{*}}
and
\psi{\upharpoonright}_{X_{\mathcal{T}_{n}}^{*}}
, where:

•

R_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}
consists of all relations from
Q
that involve only words over
X_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}
, and

•

R_{\mathcal{T}_{n}}
consists of all relations from
Q
that involve only words over
X_{\mathcal{T}_{n}}
, together with the relation
ts_{2}ts_{2}=ts_{2}t
.

It therefore suffices to show that
ts_{2}ts_{2}\sim ts_{2}t
, and for this we have

\displaystyle ts_{2}ts_{2}

\displaystyle\sim_{\eqref{P4}}ts_{2}s_{1}ts_{2}\sim_{\eqref{P1}}ts_{2}s_{1}s_{2}s_{2}ts_{2}\sim_{\eqref{P3}}ts_{1}s_{2}s_{1}s_{2}ts_{2}

\displaystyle\sim_{\eqref{P8}}ts_{1}s_{2}ts_{2}ts_{2}\sim_{\eqref{P9}}ts_{1}s_{2}s_{2}ts_{2}t\sim_{\eqref{P1}}ts_{1}ts_{2}t\sim_{\eqref{P4}}tts_{2}t\sim_{\eqref{P4}}ts_{2}t.\qed

The results of
[
23
]
and
[
34
]
mentioned in the above proof also imply that the image of
\psi
contains both
\mathcal{F}_{n}
and
\mathcal{T}_{n}
. Since
\mathcal{F}_{n}
contains
\mathcal{E}_{n}
, and since
\mathcal{T}_{n}\mathcal{E}_{n}=\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
, it follows that
\psi
is surjective. To complete the proof of Theorem
3.32
it therefore remains to show that
\ker(\psi)={\sim}
. One inclusion is easily established:

Lemma 8.2
.

We have
{\sim}\subseteq\ker(\psi)
.

Proof.

This follows from
Q\subseteq\ker(\psi)
, which is established by showing diagrammatically that
\underline{u}=\underline{v}
for every relation
(u,v)
from
Q
.
∎

The inclusion
\ker(\psi)\subseteq{\sim}
is the subject of the rest of this section.

For a word
w=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k}}\in X_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}^{*}
, we write
w^{-1}=s_{i_{k}}\cdots s_{i_{1}}
, noting that
{ww^{-1}\sim\iota\sim w^{-1}w}
by (
3.18
). For
1\leq i<j\leq n
we define words

\displaystyle c_{ij}=(s_{2}\cdots s_{j-1})(s_{1}\cdots s_{i-1}),\hskip 10.00002pt\varepsilon_{ij}=\varepsilon_{ji}=c_{ij}^{-1}ec_{ij},\hskip 10.00002pt\tau_{ij}=c_{ij}^{-1}tc_{ij}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\tau_{ji}=c_{ij}^{-1}ts_{1}c_{ij}.

Note that
c_{12}=\iota
,
\varepsilon_{12}=e
and
\tau_{12}=t
.
In Figure
2
we illustrate the partitions
\underline{\varepsilon}_{ij}=\underline{c}_{ij}^{-1}\underline{e}\ \underline{c}_{ij}
. Together with similar diagrams for
\underline{\tau}_{ij}
and
\underline{\tau}_{ji}
, it follows that

\underline{\varepsilon}_{ij}=\overline{e}_{ij}\and\underline{\tau}_{ij}=\overline{t}_{ij}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for distinct $i,j\in{\bf n}$.}

(8.3)

\underline{c}_{ij}^{-1}
1
i
j
n
\underline{e}
=
\underline{c}_{ij}
\underline{\varepsilon}_{ij}(=\overline{e}_{ij})
1
i
j
n

Figure 2:
The partition
\underline{\varepsilon}_{ij}=\underline{c}_{ij}^{-1}\underline{e}\ \underline{c}_{ij}
for
1\leq i<j\leq n
.

Lemma 8.4
.

For
w\in X_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}^{*}
, and for distinct
i,j\in{\bf n}
, we have

w^{-1}\varepsilon_{ij}w\sim\varepsilon_{i\underline{w},j\underline{w}}\and w^{-1}\tau_{ij}w\sim\tau_{i\underline{w},j\underline{w}}.

Proof.

By (both parts of) Lemma
8.1
, it suffices to show that

\underline{w}^{-1}\underline{\varepsilon}_{ij}\underline{w}=\underline{\varepsilon}_{i\underline{w},j\underline{w}}\and\underline{w}^{-1}\underline{\tau}_{ij}\underline{w}=\underline{\tau}_{i\underline{w},j\underline{w}}.

By induction, it suffices to do this in the case where
w=s_{k}
is a single letter from
X_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}
. For this we need to consider separate cases depending on the possible equalities between the elements of the sets
\{i,j\}
and
\{k,k+1\}
. Specifically, one can check diagrammatically that

\underline{s}_{k}\underline{\tau}_{ij}\underline{s}_{k}=\begin{cases}\underline{\tau}_{i+1,j}&\text{if $i=k$ and $j\not=k+1$,}\\
\underline{\tau}_{i-1,j}&\text{if $i=k+1$ and $j\not=k$,}\\
\underline{\tau}_{i,j+1}&\text{if $j=k$ and $i\not=k+1$,}\\
\underline{\tau}_{i,j-1}&\text{if $j=k+1$ and $i\not=k$,}\\
\underline{\tau}_{ji}&\text{if $\{i,j\}=\{k,k+1\}$,}\\
\underline{\tau}_{ij}&\text{if $\{i,j\}\cap\{k,k+1\}=\varnothing$,}\end{cases}

with analogous formulae for
\underline{s}_{k}\underline{\varepsilon}_{ij}\underline{s}_{k}
.
∎

We now also want to find a copy of the presentation
\operatorname{\mathsf{Sgp}}\langle X:R\rangle
for
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})
from Theorem
3.16
within
\operatorname{\mathsf{Mon}}\langle Y:Q\rangle
. For
w\in X^{+}
, we write
\overline{w}=w\phi\in\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})
. We also define a morphism

\nu:X^{+}\to Y^{*}:w\mapsto\widehat{w}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{by}\hskip 20.00003pt\widehat{e}_{ij}=\varepsilon_{ij}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\widehat{t}_{ij}=\tau_{ij}.

It follows from (
8.3
) that
\nu\psi=\phi:X^{+}\to\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})
, meaning that
\underline{\ \widehat{w}\ }=\overline{w}
for all
w\in X^{+}
.

Lemma 8.5
.

If
u,v\in X^{+}
are such that
\overline{u}=\overline{v}
, then
\widehat{u}\sim\widehat{v}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.16
,
\overline{u}=\overline{v}
implies that
u
can be transformed into
v
by a sequence of applications of relations from
R
. Thus, it suffices to prove the lemma in the case that
(u,v)
is itself a relation from
R
. If
(u,v)
is any of (
3.3
)–(
3.8
), then we note that
\widehat{u}
and
\widehat{v}
both belong to
X_{\mathcal{T}_{n}}^{*}
, so since
\underline{\ \widehat{u}\ }=\overline{u}=\overline{v}=\underline{\ \widehat{v}\ }
, we obtain
\widehat{u}\sim\widehat{v}
from part
(ii)
of Lemma
8.1
. Part
(i)
of the same lemma deals with relations (
3.9
)–(
3.11
). This leaves us to deal with (
3.12
)–(
3.15
).

(
3.12
). Here we have
\widehat{u}=\varepsilon_{ij}\tau_{ij}
and
\widehat{v}=\tau_{ij}
(for distinct
i,j\in{\bf n}
), so we need to show that
\varepsilon_{ij}\tau_{ij}\sim\tau_{ij}
. To do so, let
w\in X_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}^{*}
be such that
(1,2)\underline{w}=(i,j)
. (Here for
s,t\in{\bf n}
and
\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
we write
(s,t)\pi=(s\pi,t\pi)
, and similarly allow permutations to act on other tuples.) Then using both parts of Lemma
8.4
we have

\varepsilon_{ij}=\varepsilon_{1\underline{w},2\underline{w}}\sim w^{-1}\varepsilon_{12}w=w^{-1}ew\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and similarly}\hskip 20.00003pt\tau_{ij}\sim w^{-1}tw.

It then follows that
\varepsilon_{ij}\tau_{ij}\sim w^{-1}eww^{-1}tw\sim_{\eqref{P1}}w^{-1}etw\sim_{\eqref{P4}}w^{-1}tw\sim\tau_{ij}
.

(
3.13
). This time we fix
w\in X_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}^{*}
with
(1,2,3)\underline{w}=(i,j,k)
, and we have

\widehat{u}=\varepsilon_{jk}\tau_{ij}\sim w^{-1}\varepsilon_{23}\tau_{12}w=w^{-1}s_{1}s_{2}es_{2}s_{1}tw\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and similarly}\hskip 20.00003pt\widehat{v}\sim w^{-1}ts_{2}es_{2}w,

so we need to show that
s_{1}s_{2}es_{2}s_{1}t\sim ts_{2}es_{2}
. For this we have

s_{1}s_{2}es_{2}s_{1}t\sim_{\eqref{P4}}s_{1}s_{2}es_{2}t\sim_{\eqref{P11}}s_{1}ts_{2}es_{2}\sim_{\eqref{P4}}ts_{2}es_{2}.

(
3.14
). Taking
w\in X_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}^{*}
with
(1,2,3,4)\underline{w}=(i,j,k,l)
, we have
\tau_{ij}\sim w^{-1}\tau_{12}w=w^{-1}tw
and

\varepsilon_{kl}\sim w^{-1}\varepsilon_{34}w\sim w^{-1}(s_{2}s_{1}s_{3}s_{2})e(s_{2}s_{3}s_{1}s_{2})w\sim_{\eqref{P2}}w^{-1}(s_{2}s_{3}s_{1}s_{2})e(s_{2}s_{3}s_{1}s_{2})w,

so this time
\widehat{u}\sim\widehat{v}
follows from (
3.31
), which says that
(s_{2}s_{3}s_{1}s_{2})e(s_{2}s_{3}s_{1}s_{2})t\sim t(s_{2}s_{3}s_{1}s_{2})e(s_{2}s_{3}s_{1}s_{2})
.

(
3.15
). The proof here is almost identical to that of (
3.12
).
∎

The next lemma will be used to show that any word over
Y
with at least one occurrence of
e
or
t
is
\sim
-equivalent to an element of
\operatorname{im}(\nu)
.

Lemma 8.6
.

If
i,j\in{\bf n}
are distinct, and if
1\leq k<n
, then

(i)

\tau_{ij}s_{k}\sim u
and
s_{k}\tau_{ij}\sim v
for some
u,v\in\operatorname{im}(\nu)
,

(ii)

\varepsilon_{ij}s_{k}\sim u
and
s_{k}\varepsilon_{ij}\sim v
for some
u,v\in\operatorname{im}(\nu)
.

Proof.

(i)
. Since
\underline{\tau}_{ij}\underline{s}_{k}\in\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})
we have
\underline{\tau}_{ij}\underline{s}_{k}=\underline{\tau}_{p_{1}q_{1}}\cdots\underline{\tau}_{p_{l}q_{l}}
for suitable
p_{t},q_{t}\in{\bf n}
. It then follows from Lemma
8.1
(ii)
that
\tau_{ij}s_{k}\sim\tau_{p_{1}q_{1}}\cdots\tau_{p_{l}q_{l}}
.
The proof for
s_{k}\tau_{ij}
is essentially identical.

(ii)
. By part
(i)
we have
s_{k}\tau_{ij}\sim w
for some
w\in\operatorname{im}(\nu)
. Since
\varepsilon_{ij}\sim\tau_{ij}\varepsilon_{ij}
, as shown in the proof of Lemma
8.5
, it follows that
s_{k}\varepsilon_{ij}\sim s_{k}\tau_{ij}\varepsilon_{ij}\sim w\varepsilon_{ij}
, so we can take
v=w\varepsilon_{ij}
.

We also have
\varepsilon_{ij}s_{k}\sim_{\eqref{P1}}s_{k}s_{k}\varepsilon_{ij}s_{k}\sim s_{k}\varepsilon_{i\underline{s}_{k},j\underline{s}_{k}}
(using Lemma
8.4
in the last step), and by the previous paragraph, this is
\sim
-equivalent to a word in
\operatorname{im}(\nu)
.
∎

In the next proof, we write
\langle\Sigma\rangle
for the subsemigroup of
Y^{*}
generated by a subset
\Sigma\subseteq Y^{*}
.

Lemma 8.7
.

If
u\in Y^{*}\setminus X_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}^{*}
, then
u\sim v
for some
v\in\operatorname{im}(\nu)
.

Proof.

Let
\Sigma=X\nu=\{\varepsilon_{ij}=\varepsilon_{ji}:1\leq i<j\leq n\}\cup\{\tau_{ij},\tau_{ji}:1\leq i<j\leq n\}
, noting that
\operatorname{im}(\nu)=\langle\Sigma\rangle
. Since
e=\varepsilon_{12}
and
t=\tau_{12}
both belong to
\Sigma
, we have
Y^{*}\setminus X_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}^{*}\subseteq\langle\Sigma\cup X_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}\rangle
. Thus, we can prove the lemma by showing that any element
u
of
\langle\Sigma\cup X_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}\rangle\setminus X_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}^{*}
is
\sim
-equivalent to an element of
\langle\Sigma\rangle
. For this, we write
u=z_{1}\cdots z_{k}
, where each
z_{i}\in\Sigma\cup X_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}
, and we use Lemma
8.6
and a simple induction on the number of terms
z_{i}
that belong to
X_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}
.
∎

We can now tie together the loose ends.

Proof of Theorem
3.32
.

It remains to show that
\ker(\psi)\subseteq{\sim}
, so let
(u,u^{\prime})\in\ker(\psi)
, and write
a=\underline{u}=\underline{u}^{\prime}
. If
a\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
then we must have
u,u^{\prime}\in X_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}^{*}
(as
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}})=\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{n}
is an ideal of
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
), and then
u\sim u^{\prime}
by either part of Lemma
8.1
. So we now assume that
a\not\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
, in which case
u,u^{\prime}\not\in X_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}^{*}
, so it follows from Lemma
8.7
that
u\sim v
and
u^{\prime}\sim v^{\prime}
for some
v,v^{\prime}\in\operatorname{im}(\nu)
. By definition, this means that
v=\widehat{w}
and
v^{\prime}=\widehat{w}^{\prime}
for some
w,w^{\prime}\in X^{+}
. Since
{\sim}\subseteq\ker(\psi)
by Lemma
8.2
, we also have

\overline{w}=\underline{\ \widehat{w}\ }=\underline{v}=\underline{u}=\underline{u}^{\prime}=\underline{v}^{\prime}=\underline{\ \widehat{w}^{\prime}\ }=\overline{w}^{\prime},

so it follows from Lemma
8.5
that
\widehat{w}\sim\widehat{w}^{\prime}
. Thus,
u\sim v=\widehat{w}\sim\widehat{w}^{\prime}=v^{\prime}\sim u^{\prime}
.
∎

9
Presentation for
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}

In this section we prove Theorem
3.46
, which gives a presentation for the planar full-domain partition monoid
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
. The proof is much more involved than that of Theorems
3.16
and
3.32
. It utilises Theorem
4.9
, and a certain strong (right) action pair in
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
. One might wonder if this is simply the ‘planar analogue’ of the action pair
(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{T}_{n})
in the monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}=\mathcal{T}_{n}\mathcal{E}_{n}
. Specifically, one might wonder if
(\mathscr{P}\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathscr{P}\mathcal{T}_{n})
is an action pair in
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
, and/or if we have the product decomposition
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}=\mathscr{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}\cdot\mathscr{P}\mathcal{E}_{n}
. Both of these turn out not to be the case. Indeed, while the pair
(\mathscr{P}\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathscr{P}\mathcal{T}_{n})
satisfies
(A2)
(by virtue of
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{E}_{n}
and
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}
being submonoids of
\mathcal{E}_{n}
and
\mathcal{T}_{n}
, which do form an action pair in
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
), it does not satisfy
(A1)
for
n\geq 3
. For example, consider

}}\in\mathscr{P}\mathcal{T}_{3}

and
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}
do not lead to a product decomposition for

\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
. Indeed, Theorem
3.46
claims (among other things) that

}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $1\leq i<n$,}

and we have
\overline{f}_{i},\overline{g}_{i}\in\mathscr{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}
and
\overline{h}_{i}\in\mathscr{P}\mathcal{E}_{n}
. One might wonder how such elements could generate partitions from
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
with nested lower blocks, such as

}}\in\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{5}^{\textup{fd}}
. But for this example we have

=
=
a
,

which leads to the factorisation
a=\overline{f}_{4}\overline{f}_{3}\overline{f}_{2}\overline{f}_{1}\cdot\overline{h}_{3}\cdot\overline{f}_{2}\overline{g}_{4}\cdot\overline{h}_{3}
.

As we will see, however, the planar submonoid
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}

is
part of an action pair in
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
. Before we discuss this, we note that in fact
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}=\mathcal{O}_{n}
is (an isomorphic copy of) the monoid of order-preserving transformations of
{\bf n}
, as introduced in Section
2.1
. The pair
(\mathcal{D}_{n},\mathcal{O}_{n})
we use here will involve a new submonoid
\mathcal{D}_{n}
. The definition of
\mathcal{D}_{n}
is somewhat involved, and will be given in Section
9.1
, where we also prove a number of useful technical results, including a natural generating set. Although
\mathcal{D}_{n}
is not a semilattice, it is still a band, indeed a
right regular
band, meaning that it satisfies the identity
xyx=yx
; equivalently, it is
\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}
-trivial. We then give a presentation for
\mathcal{D}_{n}
in Section
9.2
, and verify that
(\mathcal{D}_{n},\mathcal{O}_{n})
is indeed an action pair with
\mathcal{O}_{n}\mathcal{D}_{n}=\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
in Section
9.3
. We then apply Theorem
4.9
to obtain a presentation for
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
in Section
9.4
in terms of a large generating set, and then use this to prove Theorem
3.46
. Finally, we make some observations on the structure of
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
in Section
9.5
, showing in particular that it is an example of a
grrac monoid
, in the sense of Branco, Gomes and Gould
[
8
]
.

9.1
The monoid
\mathcal{D}_{n}

We begin with some terminology and notation concerning planar partitions, and their (co)kernels.
Consider non-empty subsets
A=\{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\}
and
B=\{j_{1}<\cdots<j_{l}\}
of
{\bf n}
. We write
A<B
if
i_{k}<j_{1}
. If
A<B
or
B<A
, then we say that
A
and
B
are
separated
. We write
A\prec B
, and say that
A
is
nested
by
B
, if we have
j_{t}<i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}<j_{t+1}
for some
1\leq t<l
. If
A\prec B
or
B\prec A
, we say that
A
and
B
are
nested
. The immediate relevance of these concepts is contained in the following result concerning the planar partition monoid
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
, which is part of
[
22
, Lemma 7.1]
. In what follows, we will often use this lemma without explicit reference.

Lemma 9.1
.

If
a=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&D_{1}&\cdots&D_{t}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\in\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
, with
\min(A_{1})<\cdots<\min(A_{r})
, then

(i)

A_{1}<\cdots<A_{r}
and
B_{1}<\cdots<B_{r}
,

(ii)

for all
1\leq i<j\leq s
,
C_{i}
and
C_{j}
are either nested or separated,

(iii)

for all
1\leq i<j\leq t
,
D_{i}
and
D_{j}
are either nested or separated,

(iv)

for all
1\leq i\leq r
and
1\leq j\leq s
, either
A_{i}
and
C_{j}
are separated or else
C_{j}
is nested by
A_{i}
,

(v)

for all
1\leq i\leq r
and
1\leq j\leq t
, either
B_{i}
and
D_{j}
are separated or else
D_{j}
is nested by
B_{i}
.

An equivalence relation
\eta\in\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
is called
planar
if every pair of
\eta
-classes is either separated or nested. It follows quickly from Lemma
9.1
that these are precisely the kernels and cokernels of planar partitions. They are also the cokernels of planar
full-domain
partitions, as we will see below. It also follows from Lemma
9.1
that an equivalence
\eta\in\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
is the
kernel
of a planar full-domain partition if and only if it is
convex
, in the sense that every
\eta
-class is an interval. This is equivalent to every pair of
\eta
-classes being separated.

We write
\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
for the set of all planar equivalences on
{\bf n}
. Note that
\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
is not a
\vee
-submonoid of
\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
(for
n\geq 4
), as
\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
contains the generating set
\{\eta_{ij}:1\leq i<j\leq n\}
for
\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
.
On the other hand, the set of all convex equivalences of
{\bf n}
is a submonoid, and is isomorphic to
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{E}_{n}=\mathcal{E}_{n}\cap\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
.

Consider a planar equivalence
\eta\in\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
. Let the un-nested
\eta
-classes be
{B_{1}<\cdots<B_{r}}
, and let the remaining
\eta
-classes be
C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}
. (So each
C_{i}
is nested by some
B_{j}
, but there may also be nesting among the
C_{i}
themselves.) For each
1\leq i\leq r
, let
x_{i}=\min(B_{i})
and
y_{i}=\max(B_{i})
, and note that

x_{1}=1,\hskip 20.00003ptx_{i}=y_{i-1}+1\text{ for $2\leq i\leq r$}\and y_{r}=n.

We then define the intervals
A_{i}=[x_{i},y_{i}]=\{x_{i},x_{i}+1,\ldots,y_{i}\}
, and the partition

d_{\eta}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\in\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}.

It is easy to see that
d_{\eta}
is an idempotent.
As an example, if
\eta\in\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{8}
is such that

{\bf 8}/\eta=\big\{\{1,5,6\},\{2,3\},\{4\},\{7,8\}\big\},\hskip 20.00003pt\text{then}\hskip 20.00003ptd_{\eta}={\lower 6.02773pt\hbox{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}.

(9.2)

It is immediate from the construction that

\operatorname{coker}(d_{\eta})=\eta\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $\eta\in\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}$.}

The kernel of
d_{\eta}
is not necessarily
\eta
, though. We will denote this kernel by
\widehat{\eta}=\ker(d_{\eta})
. With the above notation, we have
{\bf n}/\widehat{\eta}=\{A_{1},\ldots,A_{r}\}=\big\{[x_{1},y_{1}],\ldots,[x_{r},y_{r}]\big\}
.

We now define the set

\mathcal{D}_{n}=\{d_{\eta}:\eta\in\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}\}.

We will show that
\mathcal{D}_{n}
is a submonoid of
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
by a somewhat indirect method, which will also allow us to establish a natural generating set. This will in turn feed into a presentation in Section
9.2
. For now it is at least clear that
\mathcal{D}_{n}
is a subset of
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
, and contains the identity element
1=d_{\Delta_{\bf n}}
. The next lemma gives an alternative characterisation of the elements of
\mathcal{D}_{n}
.

Lemma 9.3
.

Let
a\in\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
. Then
a\in\mathcal{D}_{n}
if and only if every transversal
A\cup B^{\prime}
of
a
satisfies
\min(A)=\min(B)
and
\max(A)=\max(B)
, in which case we have
a=d_{\eta}
, where
\eta=\operatorname{coker}(a)
.

Proof.

The forward implication follows immediately from the definition of the elements
d_{\eta}
. Conversely, suppose
a=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\in\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
satisfies the stated condition on transversals, assuming that
A_{1}<\cdots<A_{r}
, and let
\eta=\operatorname{coker}(a)
. We see then that
B_{1},\ldots,B_{r}
are precisely the un-nested
\eta
-classes, and it then follows by definition that
a=d_{\eta}\in\mathcal{D}_{n}
.
∎

For
1\leq i<j\leq n
, we define the partition

\overline{h}_{ij}=d_{\eta_{ij}}={\lower 6.02773pt\hbox{
