}}.

Our first main aim is to show that
\mathcal{D}_{n}
is a monoid, and that these elements
\overline{h}_{ij}
generate it. Working towards this, we start by introducing some notation that will be used frequently.

For a subset
A=\{x_{1}<\cdots<x_{k}\}\subseteq{\bf n}
, and for
1\leq i\leq k
, we define the
A
-successor of
x_{i}
to be

\operatorname{suc}_{A}(x_{i})=\begin{cases}x_{i+1}&\text{if $i<k$,}\\
x_{k}&\text{if $i=k$.}\end{cases}

For an equivalence
\eta\in\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
and for
x\in{\bf n}
, we define the
\eta
-successor of
x
to be

\operatorname{suc}_{\eta}(x)=\operatorname{suc}_{[x]_{\eta}}(x).

It is clear that for
\eta,\mu\in\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
we have

\eta=\mu\hskip 20.00003pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 20.00003pt\operatorname{suc}_{\eta}(x)=\operatorname{suc}_{\mu}(x)\text{ for all $x\in{\bf n}$.}

The next lemma uses successors to describe the situation when we left-multiply an element of
\mathcal{D}_{n}
with a partition of the form
\overline{h}_{ij}
. The statement also uses the equivalences
\widehat{\eta}=\ker(d_{\eta})
.

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
\eta\in\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
and
1\leq i<j\leq n
, put
p=\max[i]_{\widehat{\eta}}
and
q=\min[j]_{\widehat{\eta}}
, and let
\mu=\eta\vee\eta_{pq}
. Then

(i)

\mu\in\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
, and
\overline{h}_{ij}d_{\eta}=d_{\mu}
,

(ii)

if
\mu\not=\eta
, then for any
x\in{\bf n}
we have

\operatorname{suc}_{\mu}(x)=\begin{cases}q&\text{if $x=p$,}\\
\operatorname{suc}_{\eta}(x)&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proof.

(i)
.
Write
d_{\eta}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
, where
A_{1}<\cdots<A_{r}
, and assume that
i\in A_{k}
and
j\in A_{l}
. Since
i<j
, we have
k\leq l
. Using Lemma
9.3
, we have

p=\max[i]_{\widehat{\eta}}=\max(A_{k})=\max(B_{k}),\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and similarly}\hskip 20.00003ptq=\min(B_{l}).

Consequently,
B_{k}\cup B_{l}
is a
\mu=\eta\vee\eta_{pq}
-class, and the remaining
\mu
-classes are all
\eta
-classes. Since
B_{k}
and
B_{l}
are un-nested
\eta
-classes, it is easy to see that
\mu
is planar.

Now consider the product graph
\Pi(\overline{h}_{ij},d_{\eta})
, and note that the only non-trivial connection in the middle row coming from
\overline{h}_{ij}
is
\{i^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime}\}
. We now consider two cases.

Case 1.
Suppose first that
k=l
. Here it is easy to see that
\overline{h}_{ij}d_{\eta}=d_{\eta}
. Moreover, in this case
p
and
q
belong to the same
\eta
-class (i.e.
B_{k}
), and so
\mu=\eta\vee\eta_{pq}=\eta
. See Figure
3
(top) for a schematic illustration of this case.

Case 2.
Now we assume that
k<l
. Again considering the product graph
\Pi(\overline{h}_{ij},d_{\eta})
, the connection
\{i^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime}\}
from
\overline{h}_{ij}
joins the blocks
A_{k}^{\prime\prime}\cup B_{k}^{\prime}
and
A_{l}^{\prime\prime}\cup B_{l}^{\prime}
, and we see that

\overline{h}_{ij}d_{\eta}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{k-1}&A_{k}\cup\cdots\cup A_{l}&A_{l+1}&\cdots&A_{r}&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{k-1}&B_{k}\cup B_{l}&B_{l+1}&\cdots&B_{r}&B_{k+1}&\cdots&B_{l-1}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

See Figure
3
(bottom). It is easy to see that this partition satisfies the conditions of Lemma
9.3
, so we have
\overline{h}_{ij}d_{\eta}=d_{\nu}
by that lemma, where
\nu=\operatorname{coker}(\overline{h}_{ij}d_{\eta})
. But
\nu
is obtained from
\eta
by joining the
\eta
-classes
B_{k}
and
B_{l}
into a single
\nu
-class (and leaving the other
\eta
-classes intact). Since
p\in B_{k}
and
q\in B_{l}
, this means that
\nu
is exactly
\eta\vee\eta_{pq}=\mu
.

(ii)
. We have already noted that when
\mu
is not equal to
\eta
, it is obtained by replacing the two
\eta
-classes
B_{k}
and
B_{l}
by the single
\mu
-class
B_{k}\cup B_{l}
. Thus, the only
x\in{\bf n}
for which
\operatorname{suc}_{\eta}(x)\not=\operatorname{suc}_{\mu}(x)
is
x=\max(B_{k})=p
, and hence
\operatorname{suc}_{\mu}(p)=\min(B_{l})=q
.
∎

d_{\eta}
A_{k}=A_{l}
q
i
j
p
\overline{h}_{ij}
d_{\eta}
p
q
A_{k}
A_{l}
i
j
\overline{h}_{ij}

Figure 3:
Schematic illustrations of products
\overline{h}_{ij}d_{\eta}
from the proof of Lemma
9.4
, in the cases
{k=l}
(top) and
k<l
(bottom). Only the transversals of
d_{\eta}
are shown. See the proof for more details.

It is convenient at this point to define an abstract alphabet

X_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}=\{h_{ij}:1\leq i<j\leq n\}.

For a word
w=h_{i_{1}j_{1}}\cdots h_{i_{k}j_{k}}\in X_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}^{*}
we write
\overline{w}=\overline{h}_{i_{1}j_{1}}\cdots\overline{h}_{i_{k}j_{k}}
. In what follows, we will be interested in a specific family of words over
\mathcal{D}_{n}
. In order to define these, we will first extend the
h_{ij}
notation, by defining

h_{ii}=\iota\and\overline{h}_{ii}=1\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $1\leq i\leq n$.}

For a planar equivalence
\eta\in\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
we define the word

w_{\eta}=h_{1k_{1}}\cdots h_{nk_{n}},\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptk_{x}=\operatorname{suc}_{\eta}(x)\text{ for all $x\in{\bf n}$.}

For example, with
\eta\in\mathcal{D}_{8}
as in (
9.2
), we have

w_{\eta}=h_{15}h_{23}h_{33}h_{44}h_{56}h_{66}h_{78}h_{88}=h_{15}h_{23}h_{56}h_{78}.

(9.5)

Lemma 9.6
.

For any
\eta\in\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
we have
d_{\eta}=\overline{w}_{\eta}
.

Proof.

We use descending induction on
m=|{\bf n}/\eta|
. When
m=n
, we have
\eta=\Delta_{\bf n}
, in which case
d_{\eta}=1
and
w_{\eta}=h_{11}\cdots h_{nn}=\iota
, so the result is clear. We now assume that
m<n
. Let

i={\min}\big\{x\in{\bf n}:[x]_{\eta}\not=\{x\}\big\}.

Since each of
1,\ldots,i-1
belongs to a singleton
\eta
-class, we have
h_{1k_{1}}=\cdots=h_{i-1,k_{i-1}}=\iota
, and so

w_{\eta}=h_{ik_{i}}\cdots h_{nk_{n}},\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptk_{x}=\operatorname{suc}_{\eta}(x)\text{ for each $i\leq x\leq n$.}

Let
\mu\in\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
be obtained from
\eta
by replacing the class
[i]_{\eta}
by
\{i\}
and
[i]_{\eta}\setminus\{i\}
. We then have

\displaystyle d_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\overline{w}_{\mu}

by induction, as
|{\bf n}/\mu|=m+1

\displaystyle=\overline{h}_{i+1,l_{i+1}}\cdots\overline{h}_{nl_{n}}

where each
l_{i}=\operatorname{suc}_{\mu}(x)

\displaystyle=\overline{h}_{i+1,k_{i+1}}\cdots\overline{h}_{nk_{n}}

by Lemma
9.4
(ii)
, as
\eta=\mu\vee\eta_{ik_{i}}
.

It follows that

\overline{w}_{\eta}=\overline{h}_{ik_{i}}\cdot\overline{h}_{i+1,k_{i+1}}\cdots h_{nk_{n}}=\overline{h}_{ik_{i}}d_{\mu}.

By Lemma
9.4
(i)
, we have
\overline{h}_{ik_{i}}d_{\mu}=d_{\nu}
, where
\nu=\mu\vee\eta_{pq}
for

p=\max[i]_{\widehat{\mu}}=i\and q=\min[k_{i}]_{\widehat{\mu}}=k_{i}.

But this says exactly that
\nu=\mu\vee\eta_{ik_{i}}=\eta
, so that
d_{\eta}=d_{\nu}=\overline{h}_{ik_{i}}d_{\mu}=\overline{w}_{\eta}
, as required.
∎

As an example, consider again
\eta\in\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{8}
from (
9.2
). We then have the following factorisation, verifying that
d_{\eta}=\overline{w}_{\eta}
; cf (
9.5
).

=
=
d_{\eta}
\overline{h}_{15}
\overline{h}_{23}
\overline{h}_{56}
,
\overline{h}_{78}

For the next statement, recall that a band
S
is
right regular
if
xyx=yx
for all
x,y\in S
. This is equivalent to
S
being
\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}
-trivial, i.e. satisfying
x\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}y\ \Rightarrow\ x=y
.

Proposition 9.7
.

\mathcal{D}_{n}
is a submonoid of
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
, and is in fact a right regular band. Moreover, we have

\mathcal{D}_{n}=\langle\overline{h}_{ij}:1\leq i<j\leq n\rangle.

Proof.

The inclusion
\mathcal{D}_{n}\subseteq\langle\overline{h}_{ij}:1\leq i<j\leq n\rangle
follows from Lemma
9.6
, and the backward inclusion from Lemma
9.4
(i)
and a simple induction. Thus
\mathcal{D}_{n}=\langle\overline{h}_{ij}:1\leq i<j\leq n\rangle
, which also of course shows that
\mathcal{D}_{n}
is a submonoid of
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
.

To demonstrate
\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}
-triviality, let
\eta,\mu\in\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
. Then using the analogue of (
6.9
) for the
\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}
-relation, we have

d_{\eta}\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}d_{\mu}\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\operatorname{coker}(d_{\eta})=\operatorname{coker}(d_{\mu})\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\eta=\mu\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptd_{\eta}=d_{\mu}.\qed

9.2
Presentation for
\mathcal{D}_{n}

We continue to fix the alphabet

X_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}=\{h_{ij}:1\leq i<j\leq n\}.

By Proposition
9.7
, we have a surmorphism

\delta:X_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}^{*}\to\mathcal{D}_{n}:h_{ij}\mapsto\overline{h}_{ij}.

We now let
R_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}
be the set of the following relations over
X_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}
:

\displaystyle h_{ij}h_{kl}

\displaystyle=h_{kl}

if
k\leq i<j\leq l
,

(9.8)

\displaystyle h_{ij}h_{kl}

\displaystyle=h_{kl}h_{ij}

if
i<j\leq k<l
,

(9.9)

\displaystyle h_{ij}h_{jk}=h_{ik}h_{ij}

\displaystyle=h_{ik}h_{jk}

if
i<j<k
.

(9.10)

Note that (
9.8
) includes as a special case the relations

h_{ij}^{2}=h_{ij}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $1\leq i<j\leq n$.}

Our main goal of this section is to prove the following:

Theorem 9.11
.

The monoid
\mathcal{D}_{n}
has presentation
\operatorname{\mathsf{Mon}}\langle X_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}:R_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\rangle
via
\delta
.

It is easy to check that
R_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\subseteq\ker(\delta)
. For example, we verify relation (
9.10
) in Figure
4
.

i
j
k
\overline{h}_{ij}
\overline{h}_{jk}
i
j
k
\overline{h}_{ik}
\overline{h}_{ij}
i
j
k
\overline{h}_{ik}
\overline{h}_{jk}
i
j
k

Figure 4:
Diagrammatic verification of relation (
9.10
).
Top row: the product graphs
\Pi(\overline{h}_{ij},\overline{h}_{jk})
,
\Pi(\overline{h}_{ik},\overline{h}_{ij})
and
\Pi(\overline{h}_{ik},\overline{h}_{jk})
.
Bottom row: the element
\overline{h}_{ij}\overline{h}_{jk}=\overline{h}_{ik}\overline{h}_{ij}=\overline{h}_{ik}\overline{h}_{jk}
. In all diagrams we have pictured only vertices
i,\ldots,k
, and their (double-)dashed counterparts.

As ever, the inclusion
\ker(\delta)\subseteq R_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}^{\sharp}
is harder to establish.
We begin by establishing some simple consequences of the relations (
9.8
)–(
9.10
). Recall the convention that
h_{ii}=\iota
for all
i
. Throughout this section we write
{\sim}=R_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}^{\sharp}
. There should be no confusion with our earlier use of this symbol.

Lemma 9.12
.

For
i,j,k,l\in{\bf n}
we have

\displaystyle h_{ij}h_{kl}

\displaystyle\sim h_{kl}

if
k\leq i\leq j\leq l
,

(9.13)

\displaystyle h_{ij}h_{kl}

\displaystyle\sim h_{kl}h_{ij}

if
i\leq j\leq k\leq l
,

(9.14)

\displaystyle h_{ij}h_{jk}

\displaystyle\sim h_{ik}h_{ij}\sim h_{ik}h_{jk}

if
i\leq j\leq k
,

(9.15)

\displaystyle h_{ij}h_{kl}

\displaystyle\sim h_{lj}h_{kl}

if
k\leq i\leq l\leq j
,

(9.16)

\displaystyle h_{ij}h_{kl}

\displaystyle\sim h_{ik}h_{kl}

if
i\leq k\leq j\leq l
.

(9.17)

Proof.

(
9.13
)–(
9.15
). Note that these are just (
9.8
)–(
9.10
) with all ‘
<
’ signs replaced with ‘
\leq
’. Thus, we just have to check that in cases with equalities of subscripts not covered in (
9.8
)–(
9.10
) we have
\sim
-equivalence of the stated words. This is obvious for (
9.13
) and (
9.14
). For (
9.15
) it follows from the above-mentioned relations
h_{ij}^{2}=h_{ij}
, which are part of (
9.8
).

(
9.16
). Here we have
h_{ij}h_{kl}\sim_{\eqref{N1}}h_{ij}h_{il}h_{kl}\sim_{\eqref{N3}}h_{il}h_{lj}h_{kl}\sim_{\eqref{N2}}h_{lj}h_{il}h_{kl}\sim_{\eqref{N1}}h_{lj}h_{kl}
.

(
9.17
). This is similar to (
9.16
), but a little easier.
∎

We now prove a series of results concerning the words
w_{\eta}\in X_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}^{*}
(
\eta\in\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
) defined above, leading up to Corollary
9.26
, which shows that every word over
X_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}
is
\sim
-equivalent to some
w_{\eta}
.

So fix some
\eta\in\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
, and consider the word
w_{\eta}=h_{1k_{1}}\cdots h_{nk_{n}}
, where each
k_{x}=\operatorname{suc}_{\eta}(x)
, and let
p\in{\bf n}
be arbitrary. Define the sequence

p_{0}=p,\hskip 20.00003ptp_{1}=k_{p_{0}},\hskip 20.00003ptp_{2}=k_{p_{1}},\hskip 20.00003pt\ldots,

and suppose this terminates in
p_{l}=k_{p_{l}}
, where
p_{0},p_{1},\ldots,p_{l}
are all distinct. Also set
q=p_{l}
.

Lemma 9.18
.

With the above notation, and with
u=h_{pk_{p}}\cdots h_{qk_{q}}
, we have
u\sim h_{pq}u
.

Proof.

We use induction on
l
. If
l=0
then
q=p
, so
h_{pq}=\iota
, and the claim is trivial. So now suppose
l\geq 1
. We then have

u=h_{pk_{p}}\cdots h_{qk_{q}}=h_{p_{0}k_{p_{0}}}h_{p_{0}+1,k_{p_{0}+1}}\cdots h_{p_{1}-1,k_{p_{1}-1}}\cdot v,

(9.19)

where
v=h_{p_{1}k_{p_{1}}}\cdots h_{qk_{q}}
. By induction (considering that
p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{l}=q
is a shorter sequence of the relevant form), we have

v\sim h_{p_{1}q}v.

(9.20)

We then calculate

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle\sim h_{p_{0}k_{p_{0}}}h_{p_{0}+1,k_{p_{0}+1}}\cdots h_{p_{1}-1,k_{p_{1}-1}}\cdot h_{p_{1}q}v

by (
9.19
) and (
9.20
)

\displaystyle\sim h_{p_{0}k_{p_{0}}}h_{p_{1}q}\cdot h_{p_{0}+1,k_{p_{0}+1}}\cdots h_{p_{1}-1,k_{p_{1}-1}}\cdot v

by (
9.9
).

(9.21)

In this last step, relation (
9.9
) applies because
k_{i}<p_{1}
for all
p_{0}+1\leq i\leq p_{1}-1
, since the points
p_{0}+1,\ldots,p_{1}-1
are nested by the block of
\eta
containing
p_{0}
and
p_{1}=k_{p_{0}}=\operatorname{suc}_{\eta}(p_{0})
. Keeping in mind that
k_{p_{0}}=p_{1}
, we then continue:

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle\sim h_{p_{0}p_{1}}h_{p_{1}q}\cdot h_{p_{0}+1,k_{p_{0}+1}}\cdots h_{p_{1}-1,k_{p_{1}-1}}\cdot v

by (
9.21
)

\displaystyle\sim h_{p_{0}q}h_{p_{0}p_{1}}\cdot h_{p_{0}+1,k_{p_{0}+1}}\cdots h_{p_{1}-1,k_{p_{1}-1}}\cdot v

by (
9.15
)

\displaystyle=h_{pq}h_{p_{0}k_{p_{0}}}\cdot h_{p_{0}+1,k_{p_{0}+1}}\cdots h_{p_{1}-1,k_{p_{1}-1}}\cdot v

as
p_{0}=p
and
p_{1}=k_{p_{0}}

\displaystyle=h_{pq}u,

completing the proof.
∎

Among other things, the next lemma identifies an important case of Lemma
9.18
. To state the result, we first introduce some further terminology. Consider again
\eta\in\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
, and write

w_{\eta}=h_{1k_{1}}\cdots h_{nk_{n}}\and d_{\eta}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

For each
1\leq i\leq r
, write
A_{i}=[x_{i},y_{i}]
. We define the
bricks
of
w_{\eta}
to be the words

u_{i}=h_{x_{i}k_{x_{i}}}\cdots h_{y_{i}k_{y_{i}}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for each $1\leq i\leq r$.}

Lemma 9.22
.

Let
\eta\in\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
, and let
u_{1},\ldots,u_{r}
be the bricks of
w_{\eta}
, as above. Then

(i)

w_{\eta}=u_{1}\cdots u_{r}
,

(ii)

u_{i}\in\{h_{st}:x_{i}\leq s\leq t\leq y_{i}\}^{*}
for all
i
,

(iii)

u_{i}u_{j}\sim u_{j}u_{i}
for distinct
i,j
,

(iv)

u_{i}\sim h_{x_{i}y_{i}}u_{i}
for all
i
.

Proof.

(i)
. This follows immediately from the definition.

(ii)
. We must show that every subscript of every letter of the brick
{u_{i}=h_{x_{i}k_{x_{i}}}\cdots h_{y_{i}k_{y_{i}}}}
belongs to
A_{i}=[x_{i},y_{i}]
. This is obviously true of the
first
subscripts. Now consider
h_{jk_{j}}
, where
x_{i}\leq j\leq y_{i}
. Since
j\in[x_{i},y_{i}]=A_{i}
, we see that either:

•

j^{\prime}
belongs to the transversal
A_{i}\cup B_{i}^{\prime}
of
d_{\eta}
, or else

•

j^{\prime}
belongs to a lower-nontransversal
C_{t}^{\prime}
of
d_{\eta}
that is nested by
B_{i}^{\prime}
.

But
k_{j}=\operatorname{suc}_{\eta}(j)
also belongs to
B_{i}
or
C_{t}
(respectively), and since
B_{i}
and
C_{t}
are both contained in
A_{i}=[x_{i},y_{i}]
, we are done.

(iii)
. This follows immediately from part
(ii)
and relation (
9.9
).

(iv)
. This is a special case of Lemma
9.18
.
∎

We are now almost ready to show that every word over
X_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}
is
\sim
-equivalent to one of the form
w_{\eta}
. The next lemma provides the key step in the proof of this fact.

Lemma 9.23
.

If
\eta\in\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
and
1\leq i<j\leq n
, then
h_{ij}w_{\eta}\sim w_{\mu}
, where
\mu\in\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
is as in Lemma
9.4
.

Proof.

Write
d_{\eta}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
, assuming that
A_{1}<\cdots<A_{r}
, and
i\in A_{k}
and
j\in A_{l}
. We also write
A_{k}=[x,p]
and
A_{l}=[q,y]
, noting that

p=\max(A_{k})=\max[i]_{\widehat{\eta}}\and q=\min(A_{l})=\min[j]_{\widehat{\eta}}

have the same meaning as in Lemma
9.4
. Also let
u_{1},\ldots,u_{r}
be the bricks of
w_{\eta}
.

Case 1.
Suppose first that
k=l
, and note that
\mu=\eta
in this case, so we must show that
h_{ij}w_{\eta}\sim w_{\eta}
. Using (
9.9
) and Lemma
9.22
(ii)
, we have

h_{ij}w_{\eta}=h_{ij}u_{1}\cdots u_{r}\sim u_{1}\cdots u_{k-1}\cdot h_{ij}u_{k}\cdot u_{k+1}\cdots u_{r},

so we can complete the proof in this case by showing that
{h_{ij}u_{k}\sim u_{k}}
. But this follows from Lemma
9.22
(iv)
and (
9.8
):

h_{ij}u_{k}\sim h_{ij}h_{xp}u_{k}\sim h_{xp}u_{k}\sim u_{k}.

Case 2.
Now suppose
k\not=l
, so that
k<l
. Using Lemma
9.22
and (
9.9
), we then have

h_{ij}w_{\eta}=h_{ij}u_{1}\cdots u_{r}\sim v\cdot h_{ij}u_{k}u_{l}\cdot v^{\prime},

(9.24)

where for simplicity we write
v=u_{1}\cdots u_{k-1}
and
v^{\prime}=u_{k+1}\cdots u_{l-1}u_{l+1}\cdots u_{r}
. Next we claim that

h_{ij}u_{k}u_{l}\sim u_{k}h_{pq}u_{l}.

(9.25)

Indeed, for this we have

\displaystyle h_{ij}u_{k}u_{l}

\displaystyle\sim h_{ij}\cdot h_{xp}u_{k}\cdot h_{qy}u_{l}

by Lemma
9.22
(iv)

\displaystyle\sim h_{ij}h_{xp}h_{qy}\cdot u_{k}u_{l}

by (
9.9
) and Lemma
9.22
(ii)

\displaystyle\sim h_{pj}h_{xp}h_{qy}\cdot u_{k}u_{l}

by (
9.16
)

\displaystyle\sim h_{pj}h_{qy}h_{xp}\cdot u_{k}u_{l}

by (
9.9
)

\displaystyle\sim h_{pq}h_{qy}h_{xp}\cdot u_{k}u_{l}

by (
9.17
)

\displaystyle\sim h_{pq}\cdot h_{xp}u_{k}\cdot h_{qy}u_{l}

by (
9.9
) and Lemma
9.22
(ii)

\displaystyle\sim h_{pq}\cdot u_{k}u_{l}

by Lemma
9.22
(iv)

\displaystyle\sim u_{k}h_{pq}u_{l}

by (
9.9
) and Lemma
9.22
(ii)
.

We then continue:

\displaystyle h_{ij}w_{\eta}

\displaystyle\sim v\cdot h_{ij}u_{k}u_{l}\cdot v^{\prime}

by (
9.24
)

\displaystyle\sim v\cdot u_{k}h_{pq}u_{l}\cdot v^{\prime}

by (
9.25
)

\displaystyle=u_{1}\cdots u_{k-1}\cdot u_{k}h_{pq}u_{l}\cdot u_{k+1}\cdots u_{l-1}u_{l+1}\cdots u_{r}

\displaystyle\sim u_{1}\cdots u_{k-1}\cdot u_{k}h_{pq}\cdot u_{k+1}\cdots u_{l-1}u_{l}u_{l+1}\cdots u_{r}

by Lemma
9.22
(iii)

\displaystyle=u_{1}\cdots u_{k-1}\cdot u_{k}h_{pq}\cdot u_{k+1}\cdots u_{r},

and we claim that this last word this is precisely
w_{\mu}
.
Now,
w_{\eta}=u_{1}\cdots u_{k-1}\cdot u_{k}\cdot u_{k+1}\cdots u_{n}
and, writing
k_{i}=\operatorname{suc}_{\eta}(i)
for each
1\leq i\leq n
, we note that:

•

u_{k}=h_{xk_{x}}\cdots h_{p-1,k_{p-1}}h_{pk_{p}}=h_{xk_{x}}\cdots h_{p-1,k_{p-1}}
, as
k_{p}=p
(since
p=\max(A_{k})=\max(B_{k})
), and so

•

u_{k}h_{pq}=h_{xk_{x}}\cdots h_{p-1,k_{p-1}}h_{pq}
.

It now follows from Lemma
9.4
(ii)
that indeed
u_{1}\cdots u_{k-1}\cdot u_{k}h_{pq}\cdot u_{k+1}\cdots u_{r}=w_{\mu}
.
∎

Corollary 9.26
.

For any
w\in X_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}^{*}
, we have
w\sim w_{\eta}
, where
\eta=\operatorname{coker}(\overline{w})
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
w\sim w_{\eta}
for some
\eta\in\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
, since it will then follow from Lemma
9.6
that

\eta=\operatorname{coker}(d_{\eta})=\operatorname{coker}(\overline{w}_{\eta})=\operatorname{coker}(\overline{w}).

We do this by induction on
k
, the length of the word
w
. If
k=0
, then
w=\iota=w_{\Delta_{\bf n}}
, so now suppose
k\geq 1
. We then have
w=h_{ij}w^{\prime}
for some
1\leq i<j\leq n
, and some
w^{\prime}\in X_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}^{*}
of length
k-1
. By induction, we have
w^{\prime}\sim w_{\eta}
for some
\eta\in\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
, and it then follows from Lemma
9.23
that

w=h_{ij}w^{\prime}\sim h_{ij}w_{\eta}\sim w_{\mu},

for some
\mu\in\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
9.11
.

We have already observed that
\delta
is surjective, and that
{\sim}\subseteq\ker(\delta)
. For the reverse inclusion, suppose
(u,v)\in\ker(\delta)
, so that
u,v\in X_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}^{*}
and
\overline{u}=\overline{v}
. By Corollary
9.26
, we have
u\sim w_{\eta}
, where
\eta=\operatorname{coker}(\overline{u})
. But since
\overline{u}=\overline{v}
, we also have
v\sim w_{\eta}
by the same corollary, so
u\sim w_{\eta}\sim v
.
∎

9.3
The action pair
(\mathcal{D}_{n},\mathcal{O}_{n})

We now bring the monoid
\mathcal{O}_{n}
back into consideration, aiming to establish that
(\mathcal{D}_{n},\mathcal{O}_{n})
is a strong (right) action pair in
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
. We begin with a simple lemma. The proof uses the
R
operation from (
6.4
), even though
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
is not closed under it.

Lemma 9.27
.

For any
f\in\mathcal{O}_{n}
and
\eta\in\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
, we have
\operatorname{coker}(fd_{\eta})=\eta
.

Proof.

Since
f\in\mathcal{T}_{n}
we have
R(f)=1
, so it follows from Lemma
5.2
that
R(fd_{\eta})=R(d_{\eta})
. But then

\operatorname{id}_{\operatorname{coker}(fd_{\eta})}=R(fd_{\eta})=R(d_{\eta})=\operatorname{id}_{\operatorname{coker}(d_{\eta})}=\operatorname{id}_{\eta},

which implies the claim.
∎

Lemma 9.28
.

If
a\in\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
, and if
\eta=\operatorname{coker}(a)
, then
a=fd_{\eta}
for some
f\in\mathcal{O}_{n}
.

Proof.

Write
a=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{t}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
, where
A_{1}<\cdots<A_{r}
and
B_{1}<\cdots<B_{r}
, and note that each
B_{i}
is an un-nested
\eta
-class (but some of the
C_{i}
might be un-nested as well). In particular,
d_{\eta}
contains a transversal of the form
D_{i}\cup B_{i}^{\prime}
for each
1\leq i\leq r
, and by construction we have
D_{1}<\cdots<D_{r}
. Thus, choosing
x_{i}\in D_{i}
for each
i
, it follows that
f=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil x_{1}&\cdots&x_{r}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
belongs to
\mathcal{O}_{n}
, and it is easy to see that
{a=fd_{\eta}\in\mathcal{O}_{n}\mathcal{D}_{n}}
. (See Figure
5
for an example, with
n=20
.)
∎

a=
=
f
d_{\eta}

Figure 5:
A planar full-domain partition
a\in\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{20}^{\textup{fd}}
, and a factorisation
a=fd_{\eta}
, where
f\in\mathcal{O}_{20}
and
\eta=\operatorname{coker}(a)
, as in the proof of Lemma
9.28
.

In the next statement, recall that we write
\overline{h}_{ii}=1
for all
1\leq i\leq n
.

Lemma 9.29
.

If
1\leq i<j\leq n
and
1\leq k\leq n-1
, then

\overline{h}_{ij}\overline{f}_{k}=\begin{cases}\overline{f}_{k}\overline{h}_{i-1,j}&\text{if $k=i-1$,}\\
\overline{f}_{k}\overline{h}_{i,j-1}&\text{if $k=j-1$,}\\
\overline{f}_{k}\overline{h}_{ij}&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}\and\overline{h}_{ij}\overline{g}_{k}=\begin{cases}\overline{g}_{k}\overline{h}_{i+1,j}&\text{if $k=i$,}\\
\overline{g}_{k}\overline{h}_{i,j+1}&\text{if $k=j$,}\\
\overline{g}_{k}\overline{h}_{ij}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proof.

This can be checked directly by drawing diagrams, similar to Figure
4
.
∎

The expressions in Lemma
9.29
are not the simplest we could give, but will be useful for proving the next result. For example, we actually have
\overline{h}_{ij}\overline{f}_{k}=\overline{h}_{i-1,j}
when
k=i-1
.

Proposition 9.30
.

(i)

(\mathcal{D}_{n},\mathcal{O}_{n})
is a strong (right) action pair in
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
.

(ii)

We have the product decomposition
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}=\mathcal{O}_{n}\mathcal{D}_{n}
.

Proof.

(i)
. For
(A1)
we need to show that for all
f\in\mathcal{O}_{n}
and
u\in\mathcal{D}_{n}
, we have
uf=fv
for some
v\in\mathcal{D}_{n}
. This can be shown directly, though the argument is a little involved. But it also follows from Lemma
9.29
, and the fact that:

•

\mathcal{D}_{n}=\langle\overline{h}_{ij}:1\leq i<j\leq n\rangle
, as shown in Proposition
9.7
, and

•

\mathcal{O}_{n}=\langle\overline{f}_{k},\overline{g}_{k}:1\leq k<n\rangle
, as is well known; see for example
[
5
,
44
]
.

We write
u
as a product of
\overline{h}_{ij}
-s and
f
as a product of
\overline{f}_{k}
-s and
\overline{g}_{k}
-s, and then use Lemma
9.29
to move all
\overline{f}_{k}
-s and
\overline{g}_{k}
-s to the left.

For
(A2)
, let
f,g\in\mathcal{O}_{n}
and
\eta,\mu\in\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
. Then using Lemma
9.27
, we see that

fd_{\eta}=gd_{\mu}\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\eta=\operatorname{coker}(fd_{\eta})=\operatorname{coker}(gd_{\mu})=\mu\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptd_{\eta}=d_{\mu}.

(ii)
. The forward inclusion follows from Lemma
9.28
. The reverse is clear.
∎

9.4
Presentation for
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}

We now come to the proof of Theorem
3.46
, which will be in two stages. First we apply Theorem
4.9
, using the action pair
(\mathcal{D}_{n},\mathcal{O}_{n})
, to obtain an initial presentation for
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
. This will involve the alphabet

\{f_{i},g_{i}:1\leq i<n\}\cup\{h_{ij}:1\leq i<j\leq n\}.

Then, using an argument similar to (but slightly easier than) that of Section
8
, we will deduce from this the presentation
\operatorname{\mathsf{Mon}}\langle Z:O\rangle
stated in Theorem
3.46
.

We begin with some lemmas that will enable the application of Theorem
4.9
. The first involves the left congruences
\theta_{u}
(
u\in\mathcal{D}_{n}
). For a convex partition
\eta
of
{\bf n}
, with classes
A_{1}<\cdots<A_{r}
, let

f_{\eta}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil x_{1}&\cdots&x_{r}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\in\mathcal{O}_{n},\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptx_{i}=\min(A_{i})\text{ for each $1\leq i\leq k$.}

Lemma 9.31
.

For any
u\in\mathcal{D}_{n}
we have
\theta_{u}=(1,f_{\eta})^{\sharp}_{L}
, where
\eta=\ker(u)
.

Proof.

We must have
u=d_{\mu}
for some
\mu\in\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
with
\eta=\widehat{\mu}
, and it is easy to check that
d_{\mu}f_{\eta}=f_{\eta}
and
f_{\eta}d_{\mu}=d_{\mu}
. The result now follows from Lemma
4.4
.
∎

The next lemma will be used to verify assumption
(i)
in Theorem
4.9
.

Lemma 9.32
.

Let
V=\{\overline{h}_{i,i+1}:1\leq i<n\}
. Then for any
u\in\mathcal{D}_{n}
we have

\theta_{u}=\bigvee_{v\in V,\atop u\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}}\hskip 1.42262ptv}\theta_{v}.

Proof.

Fix some
u\in\mathcal{D}_{n}
, write
\eta=\ker(u)
, and let the
\eta
-classes be
A_{1}<\cdots<A_{r}
. Also let

I={\bf n}\setminus\{\max(A_{1}),\ldots,\max(A_{r})\}\and W=\{\overline{h}_{i,i+1}:i\in I\}.

We claim that
W
consists of precisely the elements of
V
that are
\geq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}}
-above
u
. Indeed, since each
\overline{h}_{i,i+1}
(
1\leq i<n
) is an idempotent, we have
u\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}}\overline{h}_{i,i+1}\ \Leftrightarrow\ u=\overline{h}_{i,i+1}u
, and it is easy to see that this is equivalent to having
i\in I
. By Lemma
9.31
we have

\theta_{u}=(1,f_{\eta})^{\sharp}_{L}\and\theta_{\overline{h}_{i,i+1}}=(1,\overline{f}_{i})^{\sharp}_{L}.

If
I=\{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{n-r}\}
, then
f_{\eta}=\overline{f}_{i_{n-r}}\cdots\overline{f}_{i_{1}}\in\langle\overline{f}_{i}:i\in I\rangle
, and it follows that assumptions
(i)
–
(iv)
of Lemma
4.5
are satisfied, and hence
\theta_{u}=\bigvee_{v\in W}\theta_{v}
, as required.
∎

We are now ready to apply Theorem
4.9
. We begin with:

•

the presentation
\operatorname{\mathsf{Mon}}\langle X_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}:R_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\rangle
for
\mathcal{D}_{n}
from Theorem
9.11
,

•

the presentation
\operatorname{\mathsf{Mon}}\langle X_{\mathcal{O}_{n}}:R_{\mathcal{O}_{n}}\rangle
for
\mathcal{O}_{n}
, where
X_{\mathcal{O}_{n}}=\{f_{i},g_{i}:1\leq i<n\}
, and where
R_{\mathcal{O}_{n}}
is the set of all relations from
O
involving only words over
X_{\mathcal{O}_{n}}
, namely (
3.35
), (
3.36
), (
3.38
)–(
3.40
), (
3.43
), (
3.44
), and the relevant parts of (
3.34
). This presentation for
\mathcal{O}_{n}
is stated in
[
34
, Section 3]
, where it was attributed to Aĭzenštat
[
5
]
; a more recent proof (in a more general context) can be found in
[
44
]
.

Proposition 9.33
.

The monoid
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
has presentation

\operatorname{\mathsf{Mon}}\langle X_{\mathcal{O}_{n}}\cup X_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}:R_{\mathcal{O}_{n}}\cup R_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{2}(V)\rangle

via

\Xi:(X_{\mathcal{O}_{n}}\cup X_{\mathcal{D}_{n}})^{*}\to\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}:f_{i}\mapsto\overline{f}_{i},\ g_{i}\mapsto\overline{g}_{i},\ h_{ij}\mapsto\overline{h}_{ij},

where
R_{1}
consists of relations

h_{ij}f_{k}=\begin{cases}f_{k}h_{i-1,j}&\text{if $k=i-1$,}\\
f_{k}h_{i,j-1}&\text{if $k=j-1$,}\\
f_{k}h_{ij}&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}\and h_{ij}g_{k}=\begin{cases}g_{k}h_{i+1,j}&\text{if $k=i$,}\\
g_{k}h_{i,j+1}&\text{if $k=j$,}\\
g_{k}h_{ij}&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

(9.34)

and
R_{2}(V)
consists of relations

f_{i}h_{i,i+1}=h_{i,i+1}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $1\leq i<n$.}

(9.35)

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
4.9
, using Lemma
9.32
to verify assumption
(i)
of the theorem, and Lemmas
9.29
,
9.31
and
9.32
to verify the forms of the relations
R_{1}\cup R_{2}(V)
.
∎

We now embark on the second stage of our proof of Theorem
3.46
, in which we will be aided by Proposition
9.33
. A key role in all that follows will be played by the words

\alpha_{ij}=h_{i}g_{i+1}\cdots g_{j-1}\in Z^{*}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $1\leq i<j\leq n$}.

Lemma 9.36
.

For any
1\leq i<j\leq n
we have
\alpha_{ij}\xi=\overline{h}_{ij}(=h_{ij}\Xi)
. Consequently,
\xi
is surjective.

Proof.

The first assertion is easily checked diagrammatically. The second follows because
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
is generated by
\{\overline{f}_{i},\overline{g}_{i}:1\leq i<n\}\cup\{\overline{h}_{ij}:1\leq i<j\leq n\}
, as follows from Proposition
9.33
.
∎

So we now need to show that
\ker(\xi)=O^{\sharp}
. As ever, the inclusion
O^{\sharp}\subseteq\ker(\xi)
follows by simply checking diagrammatically that
\xi
preserves each relation (
3.34
)–(
3.45
) from
O
. The rest of this section is devoted to establishing the inclusion
\ker(\xi)\subseteq O^{\sharp}
. To this end, we write
{\approx}=O^{\sharp}
. First we make the following observation, which follows from the fact that
O
contains the defining relations from the presentation
\operatorname{\mathsf{Mon}}\langle X_{\mathcal{O}_{n}}:R_{\mathcal{O}_{n}}\rangle
for
\mathcal{O}_{n}
.

Lemma 9.37
.

If
u,v\in X_{\mathcal{O}_{n}}^{*}
and
u\xi=v\xi
, then
u\approx v
.

We now prove two lemmas involving the words
\alpha_{ij}
. It will also be convenient to have an alternative expression for these words. Specifically, we define

\beta_{ij}=h_{j-1}f_{j-2}\cdots f_{i}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $1\leq i<j\leq n$.}

Lemma 9.38
.

For any
1\leq i<j\leq n
we have
\alpha_{ij}\approx\beta_{ij}
.

Proof.

We fix
1\leq i<n
, and use induction on
j\in\{i+1,\ldots,n\}
. When
j=i+1
, we have
\alpha_{i,i+1}=h_{i}=\beta_{i,i+1}
. For
i+2\leq j\leq n
, we use the inductive hypothesis, the definition of the
\alpha
and
\beta
words, and the stated relations from
O
, to calculate:

\displaystyle\alpha_{ij}=\alpha_{i,j-1}\cdot g_{j-1}\approx\beta_{i,j-1}\cdot g_{j-1}=h_{j-2}f_{j-3}\cdots f_{i}\cdot g_{j-1}

\displaystyle\approx_{\eqref{O7}}h_{j-2}g_{j-1}\cdot f_{j-3}\cdots f_{i}

\displaystyle\approx_{\eqref{O12}}h_{j-1}f_{j-2}\cdot f_{j-3}\cdots f_{i}=\beta_{ij}.\ \ \qed

Lemma 9.39
.

For
i,j,k,l\in{\bf n}
we have

\displaystyle\alpha_{ij}\alpha_{kl}

\displaystyle\approx\alpha_{kl}

if
k\leq i<j\leq l
,

(9.40)

\displaystyle\alpha_{ij}\alpha_{kl}

\displaystyle\approx\alpha_{kl}\alpha_{ij}

if
i<j\leq k<l
,

(9.41)

\displaystyle\alpha_{ij}\alpha_{jk}\approx\alpha_{ik}\alpha_{ij}

\displaystyle\approx\alpha_{ik}\alpha_{jk}

if
i<j<k
.

(9.42)

Proof.

(
9.40
). First note that by the definition of the
\alpha
and
\beta
words, and Lemma
9.38
, we have

\alpha_{kl}=\alpha_{kj}\cdot g_{j}\cdots g_{l-1}\approx\beta_{kj}\cdot g_{j}\cdots g_{l-1}=\beta_{ij}\cdot f_{i-1}\cdots f_{k}\cdot g_{j}\cdots g_{l-1}.

Thus, it suffices to show that
\alpha_{ij}\beta_{ij}\approx\beta_{ij}
. For
j=i+1
we have
\alpha_{i,i+1}\beta_{i,i+1}=h_{i}h_{i}\approx h_{i}
by (
3.34
). For
j\geq i+2
we have

\displaystyle\alpha_{ij}\beta_{ij}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{i,j-1}g_{j-1}\cdot h_{j-1}f_{j-2}\cdots f_{i}

\displaystyle\approx\beta_{i,j-1}\cdot h_{j-1}f_{j-2}\cdots f_{i}

by Lemma
9.38
and (
3.34
)

\displaystyle=h_{j-2}\cdot f_{j-3}\cdots f_{i}\cdot h_{j-1}f_{j-2}\cdots f_{i}

\displaystyle\approx h_{j-1}h_{j-2}\cdot f_{j-3}\cdots f_{i}\cdot f_{j-2}\cdots f_{i}

by (
3.37
) and (
3.41
)

\displaystyle\approx h_{j-1}h_{j-2}\cdot f_{j-2}\cdots f_{i}

by Lemma
9.37

\displaystyle\approx h_{j-1}\cdot f_{j-2}\cdots f_{i}

by (
3.34
)

\displaystyle=\beta_{ij}.

(
9.41
). By Lemma
9.38
, it suffices to show that
\alpha_{ij}\beta_{kl}\approx\beta_{kl}\alpha_{ij}
(for
i<j\leq k<l
). Now,

\alpha_{ij}=h_{i}g_{i+1}\cdots g_{j-1}\and\beta_{kl}=h_{l-1}f_{l-2}\cdots f_{k},

and relations (
3.37
) and (
3.40
)–(
3.42
) say that each letter appearing in
\alpha_{ij}
commutes with each letter appearing in
\beta_{kl}
. To verify this, it is best to consider separate cases in which
l\geq k+2
and
l=k+1
. In the latter we have
\beta_{kl}=h_{l-1}=h_{k}
.

(
9.42
). First note that

\alpha_{ik}\alpha_{jk}=\alpha_{ij}g_{j}\cdots g_{k-1}\alpha_{jk},

so we can show that
\alpha_{ik}\alpha_{jk}\approx\alpha_{ij}\alpha_{jk}
by showing that
g_{j}\cdots g_{k-1}\alpha_{jk}\approx\alpha_{jk}
, and we do this by induction on
k
. When
k=j+1
we have

g_{j}\cdots g_{k-1}\alpha_{jk}=g_{j}\cdot h_{j}\approx_{\eqref{O1}}h_{j}=\alpha_{jk}.

For
k\geq j+2
we have

\displaystyle g_{j}\cdots g_{k-2}g_{k-1}\alpha_{jk}

\displaystyle\approx g_{j}\cdots g_{k-2}g_{k-1}\beta_{jk}

by Lemma
9.38

\displaystyle=g_{j}\cdots g_{k-2}g_{k-1}\cdot h_{k-1}f_{k-2}\cdots f_{j}

\displaystyle\approx g_{j}\cdots g_{k-2}\cdot h_{k-1}f_{k-2}\cdots f_{j}

by (
3.34
)

\displaystyle=g_{j}\cdots g_{k-2}\cdot\beta_{jk}

\displaystyle\approx g_{j}\cdots g_{k-2}\cdot\alpha_{jk}

by Lemma
9.38

\displaystyle=g_{j}\cdots g_{k-2}\cdot\alpha_{j,k-1}g_{k-1}

\displaystyle\approx\alpha_{j,k-1}g_{k-1}

by induction

\displaystyle=\alpha_{jk}.

It remains to show that
\alpha_{ik}\alpha_{ij}\approx\alpha_{ij}\alpha_{jk}
, and since
\alpha_{ij}\alpha_{jk}\approx\alpha_{jk}\alpha_{ij}
by (
9.41
), it suffices to show that
\alpha_{ik}\alpha_{ij}\approx\alpha_{jk}\alpha_{ij}
, which by Lemma
9.38
is equivalent to
\beta_{ik}\beta_{ij}\approx\beta_{jk}\beta_{ij}
. But this is done in similar fashion to the proof of
\alpha_{ik}\alpha_{jk}\approx\alpha_{ij}\alpha_{jk}
above. We first note that

\beta_{ik}\beta_{ij}=\beta_{jk}f_{j-1}\cdots f_{i}\beta_{ij},

and then show by induction that
f_{j-1}\cdots f_{i}\beta_{ij}\approx\beta_{ij}
.
∎

Lemma 9.43
.

For
1\leq i<j\leq n
and
1\leq k<n
we have

\alpha_{ij}f_{k}\approx\begin{cases}f_{k}\alpha_{i-1,j}&\text{if $k=i-1$,}\\
f_{k}\alpha_{i,j-1}&\text{if $k=j-1$,}\\
f_{k}\alpha_{ij}&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}\and\alpha_{ij}g_{k}\approx\begin{cases}g_{k}\alpha_{i+1,j}&\text{if $k=i$,}\\
g_{k}\alpha_{i,j+1}&\text{if $k=j$,}\\
g_{k}\alpha_{ij}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proof.

We just prove the relations involving
f_{k}
, as those for
g_{k}
are analogous.

For
k=i-1
we first use Lemma
9.38
to calculate

\alpha_{ij}f_{i-1}\approx\beta_{ij}f_{i-1}=\beta_{i-1,j}\approx\alpha_{i-1,j}.

Since
\alpha_{i-1,j}=h_{i-1}g_{i}\cdots g_{j-1}
, it follows from (
3.34
) that
\alpha_{i-1,j}\approx f_{i-1}\alpha_{i-1,j}
.

For
k=j-1
we first deal with the
j=i+1
case. Here by (
3.34
) we have

\alpha_{ij}f_{j-1}=h_{i}f_{i}\approx f_{i}=f_{i}h_{ii}=f_{j-1}h_{i,j-1}.

On the other hand, if
j\geq i+2
then

\displaystyle\alpha_{ij}f_{j-1}

\displaystyle=h_{i}g_{i+1}\cdots g_{j-2}g_{j-1}\cdot f_{j-1}

\displaystyle\approx h_{i}g_{i+1}\cdots g_{j-2}\cdot f_{j-1}

by (
3.34
)

\displaystyle\approx f_{j-1}\cdot h_{i}g_{i+1}\cdots g_{j-2}

by (
3.40
) and (
3.41
)

\displaystyle=f_{j-1}\alpha_{i,j-1}.

When
k\not=i-1
and
k\not=j-1
, one of the following holds:

k\leq i-2,\hskip 20.00003pti\leq k\leq j-2\hskip 20.00003pt\text{or}\hskip 20.00003ptk\geq j.

In the first and third cases, (
3.40
) and (
3.41
) say that
f_{k}
commutes with each letter appearing in
\alpha_{ij}=h_{i}g_{i+1}\cdots g_{j-1}
. This leaves us to deal with the case that
i\leq k\leq j-2
. Here we first use Lemma
9.37
to calculate

\alpha_{ij}f_{k}=h_{i}\cdot g_{i+1}\cdots g_{j-1}f_{k}\approx h_{i}\cdot g_{i+1}\cdots g_{j-1}=\alpha_{ij}.

Thus, it remains to show that
\alpha_{ij}\approx f_{k}\alpha_{ij}
. This follows from (
3.34
) when
k=i
, as the first letter of
\alpha_{ij}
is
h_{i}
. When
i+1\leq k\leq j-2
we use (
3.41
) and then Lemma
9.37
to calculate

f_{k}\alpha_{ij}=f_{k}h_{i}g_{i+1}\cdots g_{j-1}\approx h_{i}f_{k}g_{i+1}\cdots g_{j-1}\approx h_{i}g_{i+1}\cdots g_{j-1}=\alpha_{ij}.\qed

We now define a morphism

(X_{\mathcal{O}_{n}}\cup X_{\mathcal{D}_{n}})^{*}\to Z^{*}:w\mapsto\widehat{w}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{by}\hskip 20.00003pt\widehat{f}_{i}=f_{i},\hskip 10.00002pt\widehat{g}_{i}=g_{i}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\widehat{h}_{ij}=\alpha_{ij}.

Lemma 9.44
.

For any
(u,v)\in\ker(\Xi)
, we have
\widehat{u}\approx\widehat{v}
.

Proof.

Since
\ker(\Xi)=(R_{\mathcal{O}_{n}}\cup R_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{2}(V))^{\sharp}
by Proposition
9.33
, we can assume that
(u,v)
is one of the relations from this set. We consider the relations of the various forms separately.

•

If
(u,v)\in R_{\mathcal{O}_{n}}
, then
\widehat{u}=u\approx v=\widehat{v}
since
O
contains
R_{\mathcal{O}_{n}}
.

•

If
(u,v)\in R_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}
, then Lemma
9.39
gives
\widehat{u}\approx\widehat{v}
.

•

Similarly, Lemma
9.43
deals with relations from
R_{1}
.

•

Finally, if
(u,v)\in R_{2}(V)
, then
\widehat{u}=f_{i}h_{i}\approx h_{i}=\widehat{v}
by (
3.34
). ∎

We are now ready to complete our objective.

Proof of Theorem
3.46
.

As already observed, it remains to show that
\ker(\xi)\subseteq{\approx}
, so let
(u,v)\in\ker(\xi)
. Let
u^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime}
be the words over
X_{\mathcal{O}_{n}}\cup X_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}
obtained by replacing any letter
h_{i}\in Z
by
h_{i,i+1}\in X_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}
. By Lemma
9.36
we have
u^{\prime}\Xi=u\xi
and
v^{\prime}\Xi=v\xi
. Since
(u,v)\in\ker(\xi)
, it follows that
(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\in\ker(\Xi)
. Lemma
9.44
then gives
\widehat{u}\;\!^{\prime}\approx\widehat{v}\;\!^{\prime}
. But
\widehat{u}\;\!^{\prime}=u
and
\widehat{v}\;\!^{\prime}=v
, and this completes the proof that
u\approx v
.
∎

9.5
The structure of
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}

Our study of the full-domain partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
made heavy use of the
D
and
R
operations given in (
6.4
), which give
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
the structure of a right restriction Ehresmann monoid. We have already observed that the planar submonoid
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
is closed under
D
but not
R
, so it does not inherit the property of being a right restriction monoid (or even an Ehresmann monoid). It does however satisfy some more general conditions with respect to a different unary range operation, which we will denote by
\rho
to distinguish it from
R
. Specifically, we define

\rho(a)=d_{\operatorname{coker}(a)}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $a\in\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}$.}

So
\rho
is a map
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}\to\mathcal{D}_{n}
. The next result shows that
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
is a so-called
grrac monoid
. This is the left-right dual of the
glrac monoids
introduced in
[
8
]
and studied further in
[
27
]
. The acronyms stand for ‘
g
eneralised
r
ight/
l
eft
r
estriction monoids possessing the
a
mple and
c
ongruence conditions’, which are
(G8)
and
(G7)
, respectively, in the following list. In the proof we again make use of the
R
operation, even though
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
is not closed under it.

Proposition 9.45
.

For all
a,b\in\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
we have:

(G1)

a\rho(a)=a
,

(G2)

\rho(\rho(a))=\rho(a)
,

(G3)

\rho(a)\rho(a)=\rho(a)
,

(G4)

\rho(a)\rho(b)\rho(a)=\rho(b)\rho(a)
,

(G5)

\rho(\rho(a)\rho(b))=\rho(a)\rho(b)
,

(G6)

\rho(ab)\rho(b)=\rho(ab)
,

(G7)

\rho(ab)=\rho(\rho(a)b)
,

(G8)

\rho(a)b=b\rho(ab)
.

Proof.

We begin with some simple observations that be used throughout the proof; these hold for any
a,b\in\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\textup{fd}}
. First, by construction, we have

\operatorname{coker}(\rho(a))=\operatorname{coker}(d_{\operatorname{coker}(a)})=\operatorname{coker}(a).

(9.46)

It follows from this that

R(\rho(a))=\operatorname{id}_{\operatorname{coker}(\rho(a))}=\operatorname{id}_{\operatorname{coker}(a)}=R(a).

(9.47)

Combining this with
(E3)
, it follows that

R(ab)=R(R(a)b)=R(R(\rho(a))b)=R(\rho(a)b).

(9.48)

(G1)
. By Lemma
9.28
, we have
a=fd_{\eta}
for some
f\in\mathcal{O}_{n}
, and where
\eta=\operatorname{coker}(a)
. Since
d_{\eta}
is an idempotent, it follows that
a=ad_{\eta}=a\rho(a)
.

(G2)
. Using (
9.46
), we have
\rho(\rho(a))=d_{\operatorname{coker}(\rho(a))}=d_{\operatorname{coker}(a)}=\rho(a)
.

(G3)
and
(G4)
. These follow from the fact that
\mathcal{D}_{n}
is a right regular band (Proposition
9.7
).

(G5)
. Since
\mathcal{D}_{n}
is a monoid, we have
\rho(a)\rho(b)=d_{\eta}
for some
\eta\in\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
, so this identity follows from
\rho(d_{\eta})=d_{\operatorname{coker}(d_{\eta})}=d_{\eta}
.

(G6)
. Since
\rho(ab)\rho(b)
and
\rho(ab)
both belong to
\mathcal{D}_{n}
we can show they are equal by showing that they have the same cokernel, and by definition this is equivalent to showing that

R(\rho(ab)\rho(b))=R(\rho(ab)).

For this we have

R(\rho(ab)\rho(b))=_{\eqref{eq:Rrhoab}}R(ab\rho(b))=_{\ref{G1}}R(ab)=_{\eqref{eq:Rrho}}R(\rho(ab)).

(G7)
. Again, we can establish this by showing that
R(\rho(ab))=R(\rho(\rho(a)b))
. By (
9.47
), this is equivalent to
R(ab)=R(\rho(a)b)
, and this is precisely (
9.48
).

(G8)
. Let
u=\rho(a)
, and note that by
(G7)
, the identity
(G8)
is equivalent to
ub=b\rho(ub)
, i.e. to

ub=bd_{\eta},\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003pt\eta=\operatorname{coker}(ub).

Now, by Lemma
9.28
, we have
b=fd_{\mu}
for some
f\in\mathcal{O}_{n}
, where
\mu=\operatorname{coker}(b)
. Since
(\mathcal{D}_{n},\mathcal{O}_{n})
is a strong action pair (Proposition
9.30
), it follows from property
(A1)
that
uf=fv
for some
v\in\mathcal{D}_{n}
. Since
\mathcal{D}_{n}
is a right regular band (Proposition
9.7
), we have
vd_{\mu}=d_{\mu}vd_{\mu}
. Also, since
\mathcal{D}_{n}
is a monoid, we have
vd_{\mu}=d_{\nu}
for some
\nu\in\mathscr{P}\mathfrak{Eq}_{n}
. Putting all of this together, it follows that

ub=ufd_{\mu}=fvd_{\mu}=fd_{\mu}vd_{\mu}=bd_{\nu}.

(9.49)

So it remains to show that
\nu=\eta
. But for this we have

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle=\operatorname{coker}(fd_{\nu})

by Lemma
9.27

\displaystyle=\operatorname{coker}(fvd_{\mu})

by definition of
\nu

\displaystyle=\operatorname{coker}(ub)

by (
9.49
)

\displaystyle=\eta.
